Shanghai next? City’s art scene leans into an uncertain future

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Since 2014, West Bund Art & Design (shown in 2025) has expanded to include multiple venues Courtesy West Bund Art & Design

Shanghai is a restless city. Its impatient hustle may spare its art scene from the plateau of middle age as it navigates a crisis of transition after the Covid years derailed its late 2010s momentum. Going into Shanghai’s annual art week (10-15 November), with two established fairs anchoring an explosion of art events across the city, its galleries, artists and institutions are assessing how the city’s art market can weather China’s economic slowdown and whether the cultural momentum of the past three decades can be sustained.

“Shanghai has so many galleries, so many museums, so many eccentric little spaces that you could come in the middle of summer and still see art non-stop,” says Lorenz Helbling, who founded ShanghArt in 1996. One of the first contemporary art galleries in China, it now has locations in Shanghai, Beijing and Singapore. The Swiss transplant says the volume of artistic activity in the city of 25 million people can be compared to New York’s, though the scene can be harder to find. “You can make a lot of fun things here.”

The city’s two large-scale fairs, Art021 Shanghai (11-14 November) and West Bund Art & Design (10-15 November), established in 2013 and 2014 respectively, set the barometer and prime the mood. So far, Shanghai’s core galleries have largely survived China’s property crash and deflationary economy, but they need young artists and new collectors to continue buoying their businesses.

Lise Li, who founded Shanghai’s Vanguard Gallery in 2004, says the city is “still in an upward phase, just growing much more slowly than before”. During art week, Li’s gallery will participate in the second iteration of the boutique fair Hangover Shanghai (11-15 November) and in Art021, while showing the artist Tang Chao in the gallery and collaborating on a group show at the artist-run 33ml Offspace. Several artists represented by Vanguard are having big moments nearby: Liao Fei will simultaneously be showing at Hangzhou’s Imagokinetics and the artist group known as Jiū Society has an exhibition at Nanjing’s Beiqiu Museum of Contemporary Art.

Established in 2013, Art021 Shanghai (pictured in 2025) is one of the most successful art fairs in the city Photo: Lisa Movius

Though the buzz of the 2010s has quieted, Li says, “there are quite a few more art institutions, and more newcomers are getting involved. The number of young artists is increasing rapidly; we see a lot of new faces every year. But most exhibitions are relatively conservative, which is rather a pity.”

The market has “changed considerably” compared to what now “might be called the ‘golden 15 years’ of China’s contemporary art market: the shift in the broader economic environment over the past few years has been impossible to ignore”, Otto Neu says. In 2022 he co-founded Nan Ke Gallery, which has a group show about art and literature on view during the fair week. “For artists and galleries alike, it is certainly a challenging moment. We probably have to work twice as hard as we did before simply to survive and keep things moving.”

Change, Neu adds, does not have to be negative. He cites the Chinese expression “great waves wash away the sand and leave the gold behind”, adding that “difficult periods can force an industry to become more focused, more rigorous and perhaps more honest about what really matters. In that sense, this may also be an important stage of maturation for Shanghai’s art scene.”

In addition to this month’s two international art fairs, Shanghai’s many resident artists—ranging from established figures like Ding Yi, Zhang Enli and Yang Fudong to mid-career and emerging talents—can rely on year-round programming at a healthy variety of homegrown and imported galleries. Western outfits like Almine Rech, Perrotin and Lisson maintain spaces in Shanghai, as do Asian powerhouses including Japan’s Ota Fine Arts and Korea’s Arario Gallery. ShanghArt has been joined by several influential local galleries, among them Antenna Space, Gallery Vacancy, Capsule Gallery and Don Gallery.

Major state-backed museums like the Power Station of Art, Museum of Art Pudong and West Bund Museum (which is partnered with the Centre Pompidou in Paris) have a complement of private museums that have proliferated since the 2010s. The Rockbund Art Museum, Long Museum and Yuz Museum are the best known, but smaller, newer, specialised or further-flung institutions like Fotografiska and the Museum of Glass, and non-profit spaces like Pond Society, Longlati and Prada Rong Zhai, have also enriched the Shanghai art ecosystem.

Vanguard Gallery’s Li credits Shanghai’s “open-city vibe” and its large base of collectors and visitors, along with the museums, galleries and artists, plus a citizenry curious “about fashion and new things, a strong history of business, and a diverse cultural structure. The main downside is that the cost of living is too high.” The lack of art information platforms, even in Chinese, is the other main challenge, she says.

The sense of openness is what drew the collector Cherry Xu to open her art-meets-fashion space Cheruby in the city last year. With new initiatives, “the boundaries between artists, designers, musicians and people working in fashion are becoming increasingly blurred”, she says, opening the art world to new audiences. “This kind of natural crossover between different fields is something very particular to Shanghai,” Xu says. “Art here doesn’t operate as an isolated system; it is constantly in conversation with design, music, fashion, technology and a broader youth culture, with these different communities influencing one another.”

Compared to Beijing’s art scene, Neu says, Shanghai’s creative community maintains a “healthy sense of distance. There is a certain independence between galleries, institutions, artists and collectors, which has shaped a rather distinctive ecosystem here”, a happy, pragmatic chaos of sorts. Beijing’s art scene is built on personal connections, Neu says, while in Shanghai, “between institutions, between galleries and even between galleries and collectors, there is often an understanding that business is business, friendship is friendship, and collaboration does not necessarily require complicated personal obligations or relationships”.

However, Neu adds that those divisions could become vulnerabilities in the current economic upheaval. “Perhaps this is precisely the moment when Shanghai’s art community needs to become a little more connected and cohesive than it has been in the past—not by giving up its independence, but by finding better ways to support one another,” he says.

Helbling says that Beijing is “more split up into scenes which work on their own”, while Shanghai has various circles that interact and are more permeable. He likens the city’s art community to its fairly compact geography, with the art neighbourhoods all within comparably easy reach of each other.

Twenty years ago, Shanghai was the upstart artistic challenger to Beijing, the political capital home to China’s leading art academy. Hong Kong’s concurrent cultural rise proved complimentary to Shanghai’s, and many in Shanghai say the expanding art activity in Korea, Japan and Singapore—as well as in mainland Chinese cities like Chengdu, Shenzhen and nearby Nanjing, Hangzhou and Suzhou—have been net positives for the local scene.

“This situation didn’t happen overnight; it’s the result of years of collaboration between Shanghai’s art institutions, galleries and fairs, as well as the collectors and artists living here,” Li says. “It’s not simply due to fairs or art weeks alone. The rise of art in new Chinese cities has also become developed, likely influenced by Shanghai and Hong Kong.”

Shanghai “was one of the earliest cities in China to develop a contemporary art market, and its relatively open culture and international outlook have given it a particular advantage”, Neu says. “Whether we are talking about collecting, aesthetic awareness or public understanding of contemporary art, Shanghai has accumulated more than two decades of experience and audience development. That kind of cultural infrastructure takes time to build.”

He adds that growing art scenes in other mainland cities represent “something very positive for Shanghai rather than a threat. A stronger regional art ecosystem ultimately creates more audiences, more conversations and more opportunities for everyone. Healthy competition and exchange between cities can only make the broader Chinese art scene more dynamic.”

Regionally, Xu says, Shanghai “has a very distinct local culture, its own audiences and creative communities, [while] its relationship with other Asian cities is becoming much more direct. It feels increasingly natural for artists, curators and other creative practitioners to live and work across different cities in Asia, and for us to look to what is happening in Tokyo, Seoul, Hong Kong, Singapore, Bangkok and elsewhere in the region, rather than instinctively treating Europe and the US as the only points of reference.” For Shanghai’s art week, Cheruby is showing a solo exhibition by the multidisciplinary Thai artist Tanat Teeradakorn.

“Shanghai has an interesting place within this network,” Xu says. “It has a relatively mature art infrastructure, but also very strong fashion, design, commercial, technological and popular cultures, and these worlds exist very close to one another in everyday life. It also has a large and increasingly young audience, so when an artist comes to Shanghai, they are encountering more than an established ‘art world’; they are entering a complex and fast-moving urban environment that is constantly being reshaped by new forms of consumption, technology and youth culture. That complexity creates a lot of possibilities.”

Zhang Ruyi, whose 2022 exhibition at Beijing’s UCCA Center for Contemporary Art is shown here, is among Shanghai’s mid-career artists to watch Photo: Sun Shi; courtesy UCCA Center for Contemporary Art

After helping to build the careers of some of Asia’s biggest art stars, Helbling now focuses on cultivating rising talents through more group shows and dialogue. “You always look at what artists are doing,” he says. “Many of them now operate between here and the West—educated here and in the West”, with networks and roots in both cultures.

After suspended mobility during the Covid years, dual-culture artists in Shanghai and across Asia have resumed their frequent travels between regions. Often the children of artists or collectors, young Chinese artists are very well versed in both Western and Asian artistic traditions, giving them a sophisticated perspective. “They have an interesting job to do to figure out what kind of art makes sense today,” Helbling says.

He recalls the “loneliness” of Shanghai’s 1990s avant-garde, now rendered “impossible” by contemporary art’s citywide popularity. Still, he views the 2000 internationalisation of the Shanghai Biennale, the first contemporary art fair SH Contemporary (2007-14), and the flowering of galleries and museums as all part of one era.

“There was from the 1990s when Shanghai opened up until the Covid time, and there is after Covid time,” he says. “The first 30 years, and now the second 30 years.”

Week-long hotel quarantines are still required of all international and Hong Kong arrivals to the mainland, deterring all but the most determined dealers

More than 100 events accompanied two major fairs, with a vibrant and chaotic energy befitting China's financial capital

Art021 and West Bund fairs, plus a biennial and a slew of gallery shows, were not dampened by the slowed economy and a precarious art market

Transfer insider outlines Liverpool’s stance on selling Frimpong amid reported Saudi interest

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Liverpool could potentially be open to selling Jeremie Frimpong for the right price in 2027, according to one transfer insider.

The Dutchman joined the Reds from Bayer Leverkusen almost 18 months ago and scored on his debut in the Community Shield, but unfortunately his time at Anfield so far has been dogged by several injury absences.

The 25-year-old has been sidelined with a muscle problem he incurred in the victory at Brentford last month, when he was forced off just seven minutes after his introduction as a half-time substitute, whilst he’s also shipped criticism over the nature of some of his performances this term.

According to Pete O’Rourke for Football Insider, Liverpool could ‘consider’ selling Frimpong next year, and the right-back has reportedly been attracting transfer interest from Saudi Pro League outfit Al-Hilal.

The Merseysiders won’t sanction a January exit for the Netherlands international due to concerns over squad depth amid the ongoing long-term absence of Conor Bradley, but club and player could be open to a summer departure if a ‘big offer’ is tabled.

The transfer insider also claimed that Andoni Iraola doesn’t have the strength in depth that he’d like, with the Basque feeling that his squad is lighter than many of the Reds’ direct rivals.

Want more Empire of the Kop coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

First things first, there’s no way that Liverpool are in a position to consider offloading Frimpong in January, even if Bradley manages to return by then.

The latter will take time to fully get up to speed after not playing for nearly a whole year, and his own injury history isn’t the most auspicious either.

Ronald Araujo is currently doing an excellent job at right-back, but it isn’t his natural position and it remains to be seen whether or not his loan move from Barcelona will be converted into a permanent transfer.

If that does happen and Bradley rediscovers the strong performance levels that he’d shown prior to his knee injury, maybe then Liverpool would have more scope to consider a substantial offer for Frimpong if he’s not getting much of a look-in under Iraola.

For now, though, the Dutchman is very much needed at Anfield, and hopefully he’ll become available for action once the November international break has concluded towards the end of next week.

How Italy can replace Zaniolo ahead of France and Belgium tests

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Nicolo Zaniolo has withdrawn from the Italy national team squad for unspecified personal reasons, which leaves Roberto Mancini with two main options to pick from on Italy’s right flank.

Zaniolo was one of 31 players originally called up by Mancini on Thursday evening, ahead of Nations League against France this Thursday and Belgium on Sunday. It was confirmed on Tuesday that the Udinese attacker had left the camp for personal reasons.

Zaniolo has been included in both Italy national team squads since the return of Mancini as CT, but has not featured for the Azzurri in either of those camps given that he was forced to withdraw through injury back in September.

As things stand in November, a replacement for Zaniolo in November has not been called up.

Zaniolo was able to train with the Italy squad on Monday, at a training session at Coverciano attended by Football Italia.

During Monday’s session, Zaniolo was tested among the three right-wing options, along with Napoli’s Antonio Vergara and Como’s Mattia Liberali. One of them could now be in line for a starting opportunity when Italy take on France in San Siro on Thursday evening.

Speaking at his press conference on Monday, Mancini praised Zaniolo for his versatility, stating that he is capable of covering in practically every position in the attack: “Zaniolo can play everywhere with his qualities. Attacking midfielder, winger or striker,” said the CT.

Adding further context behind his decision to call up Zaniolo for a second time, Mancini said: “I expect the same thing as everyone else. Perform well and be consistent. Start becoming decisive because time passes by. He used to be a kid, and now he should be a player who makes a difference with the national team.

“Back in 2018, we had more experienced players. Giorgio, Leo, Verratti, Jorginho, were the most experienced ones. Today we don’t have many.”

Peter Young is a Senior Reporter with Football Italia.

Kompany back at Bayern’s training ground after collapse

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Bayern Munich have confirmed Vincent Kompany has returned to the club's training ground for the first time since collapsing last month.

The former Manchester City defender collapsed during a training session prior to Bayern's Bundesliga win over Freiburg in late October.

It was subsequently confirmed that Kompany was in a stable condition and undergoing tests in hospital, with sporting director Max Eberl saying he could miss "weeks".

Kompany's assistant Aaron Danks took his place on the sideline for five matches, overseeing victories over Freiburg, Magdeburg, Atletico Madrid and Mainz, as well as a draw with Klassiker rivals Borussia Dortmund.

On Tuesday, Bayern confirmed in a short statement that Kompany had returned to their base at Sabener Strasse, where he is also undergoing rehabilitation alongside his work, having suffered a shoulder injury when he collapsed.

So good to see you back, boss  Vincent Kompany is on the road to recovery and has been back at the training ground at Säbener Straße since the start of the week. There has not been any announcement regarding Kompany's chances of being on the touchline for Bayern's next game, at home to Koln in the Bundesliga on November 20.

Bayern then visit Lille in the Champions League five days later, before another road trip in the Bundesliga, to face Hamburger SV.

Ring's New $250 Indoor Camera Drone Sounds Terrifying

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ring made its name with its smart doorbells with built-in cameras, and now sells more indoor cameras than any other company, capturing more than 40% of the market. When you've already moved tens of millions of devices (enough to raise serious concerns about their use as part of the surveillance state), you always need to keep innovating, and today, the company announced a product it has been working on for a decade: an indoor camera drone powered by artificial intelligence. I got a chance to check it out during a media event yesterday, and I can report that, as harbingers of our dystopian future go, it is only mildly terrifying.

The drone camera is one of two products in the company's new Ring Robotics line, alongside a "rover cam" that looks kind of like the canister of a vacuum cleaner attached to an iPad. While the Ring Rover Cam is priced at $999.99 and targeted at businesses, the Ring Drone Cam is a much more affordable $249.99, reasonable enough that it will appeal to consumers looking for another way to keep (robot) eyes on their stuff.

The drone camera records 2K video and has a 148-degree field of view and the ability to rotate 360 degrees. It works with existing Ring cameras, doorbells, sensors, and alarms, as well as the Alexa Home Map. It has a flight time of eight minutes, and once it returns to its base station, it takes around an hour to fully recharge (eight minutes on the dock will give it one minute in the air).

As Ring founder Jamie Siminoff explained during the preview event, the company has been trying to build a workable camera drone for around a decade, but couldn't overcome certain design challenges until the advancement of AI models that allow the device to handle most of the computing—from route programming to threat detection—via the cloud, so the drones can be smaller and lighter.

According to Ring, when setting up the Ring Drone, you carry it around your house, allowing it to map the interior. It will "intelligently identify rooms and suggest patrol points: spots worth extra attention." When sent out on "patrol," the drone's AI pilot will travel between hotspots, sensing and avoiding furniture and other obstacles along the way.The Ring Drone works with other Ring cameras, so if an alert is triggered somewhere in your home—say, a window opening unexpectedly—the drone will take off to investigate. It has a built-in security light that will make its presence known, so it can also act as a deterrent, though the screaming sound of its spinning propellers will accomplish that just as well.

According to Ring, the drone camera can deliver peace of mind whether you're home or away, and admittedly I do like the idea of being able to check on my pets or make sure no pipes have burst while I'm at work or on vacation (though admittedly you can get the same sort of monitoring without involving an AI device that looks like a mini version of the flying robots from The Terminator).

I admit that the Ring Drone Camera seems pretty cool, provided I don't think about it too much. I don't know that I really feel the need to have every inch of my home covered in security cameras, but I do like the idea that the drone could, for example, go to check out what's going on if one of my smoke alarms' temperature sensors reports that one spot in the house is all of a sudden hotter than it should be.

But there's something slightly unsettling to me about the idea of a robot patrolling my home while I sleep. Yes, I know that there are privacy and security features in place—Ring says the videos its robots capture is protected by its "strongest default encryption yet"—but it's also yet one more example of how comfortable we've all gotten with cameras being absolutely everywhere, to the point that people think nothing of putting them all over their homes. (This is probably why I don't own a robot vacuum.) And it doesn't help that the drone sounds absolutely terrifying, which Ring claims is by design: "If someone’s there who shouldn’t be, a flying drone overhead...makes it clear they’ve been spotted. [The] devices are built to deter, not just monitor." True enough, but there's also no getting around the fact that drone propellers are unavoidably loud, and the sound of the Ring Drone Camera reminds me of nothing more than the screeching violins in a horror movie. (Note that the drone we were shown was white, but the final version sold will be dark grey.)

If you're ready to enter this brave new world of security, you'll have to wait a bit longer: The Ring Drone Camera isn't expected to ship until January 2027, but you can sign up to be alerted when it is available for preorder now.

Man Utd’s biggest underperformers in 2026-27: Martinez 1st, Fernandes 3rd…

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Manchester United‘s disastrous start to the new season has led to pressure on Michael Carrick in the manager’s role, but it probably isn’t all his fault.

You only need to look at how well he did after taking charge last season to realise Carrick is capable of delivering results for United. But for whatever reason, they aren’t coming now.

Carrick is at risk of the sack, but many United fans are still on his side. Instead, the finger of blame has been directed towards technical director Jason Wilcox and other boardroom figures – not to mention the players.

Indeed, it’s those on the pitch who are letting United down right now. Some look like they’re throwing in the towel.

Let’s take a closer look at their WhoScored ratings (Premier League games only) to see who has declined the most this season compared to last season – according to the stats, at least.

“I expect more, 100%. I expect more from our technical director.” Maybe United should expect more from you, Lisandro.

His outburst after the Atletico Madrid loss reverberated strongly, but Martinez hasn’t been at his best either.

He was the guilty party in United’s loss to Chelsea, giving away a penalty and getting sent off. And it wasn’t his first error this season either.

In fairness, Sesko has had a delayed start to the season and is yet to start a league game, coming off the bench four times.

He won’t get to boost his ratings for a while either, as he’s expected to be injured until January.

The Slovenian striker does have one goal to his name from his four cameos so far, but questions persist over whether he’s the right striker for United in the long term.

Maybe the only way was down for United’s captain after how well he did last season.

Fernandes was the Premier League’s best player last term after breaking the record for most assists in a season.

He still ranks as United’s best player this season and the only one with an average rating above seven.

But he only has two assists to show for it so far (along with four goals) and his work is cut out to get near the benchmark he set last season.

Amad is yet to score or assist a goal in the league this season, despite starting five times.

He started the season injured and will be hoping to pick up some form in the coming weeks.

Heaven had something of a breakthrough into the United first team last season and is still on the fringes.

The young defender definitely has potential, but this might not be the right environment for him to fulfil it.

His rating of 6.23 from two starts and three sub appearances is the second lowest of anyone to start a game in the United squad at the moment, but hardly anyone would pick him out as the main culprit.

Dorgu has always felt like a square peg in a round hole for United. Is he a left-back? Is he a winger? Who knows.

He’s only played six times in the league so far this season, and with only two of those appearances being as a starter, he hasn’t had much of a chance to build up some form.

This winter will mark two years of Dorgu at United and the jury remains out over whether he’ll make it in the long term.

United had a big decision to make last summer in goal. To his credit, Lammens had a solid debut season as their first-choice keeper.

It was never a given he’d become number one once he arrived, but he put those doubts to one side. But was that just temporary?

United are yet to keep a clean sheet in the league this season, which is concerning.

Cunha had a reasonable debut season at Old Trafford, sometimes a striker, sometimes a winger, sometimes playing in behind.

He’s only scored once from 10 games in the new season, although he has got a couple of assists.

Mazraoui has only been given two starts in the league this season, coming off the bench in three other games.

There was a gap of eight league games between his two starts, which suggests he’s falling out of favour.

United’s other option at right-back has been on the decline too – in some aspects, at least.

Dalot has played some part in every game so far, but is averaging more fouls and fewer clearances than last season.

Your ‘Healthy’ Soda Alternative May Not Be So Healthy for Your Liver

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

WE'RE LONG PAST having to make a decision between just Coke or Pepsi. You've got a lot of options if you're bored of water—from fizzy nonalcoholic drinks to flavored seltzers. But while a lot of these drinks market themselves as healthy alternatives to sodas, research suggests they could be silently doing a number on your liver health.

If you've done the work to switch to diet sodas or have learned to love prebiotic sodas like Olipop or Poppi, the news can be pretty hard to hear. So it's important to get this out of the way upfront: If you have a "healthy" soda every once in a while, the experts we spoke to say the risk of having liver problems is unlikely. The real danger to your liver is if you’re tossing these back on a daily or near-daily basis. Here's everything you need to know.

THE STUDY PRESENTED at the United European Gastroenterology (UEG) Week 2025 used data from 103,251 people who enrolled in the UK Biobank and didn’t have liver disease at the start of the study. Participants were asked to fill out questionnaires about what they ate and drank throughout the day. The researchers then paid close attention to how much sugar-sweetened and low- or non-sugar-sweetened beverages they drank.

The major takeaway from the study is that it pretty much linked any drink that wasn’t water to metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD). You might know it by its former name, nonalcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD). But whatever you call it, it's no joke.

MASLD happens when too much fat builds up in your liver from inputs other than alcohol. It can lead to serious liver scarring or end-stage liver disease. It can also put you at high risk for heart disease and diabetes. According to the American College of Gastroenterology, heart disease is the number one killer of people living with MASLD.

DAILY DRINKING OF nearly 9 ounces of any type of drink that was not water was associated with a significantly higher risk of developing MASLD. That risk jumped 50 percent for sugar-sweetened beverages and was surprisingly higher—60 percent—for those who drank low- or non-sugar-sweetened beverages. (Though some of these prebiotic sodas have more sugar and less fiber than marketing claims might imply).

After a 10-year follow-up, 949 people developed MASLD and 103 died from liver-related causes. Both types of beverages were significantly connected to increases in liver fat.

Replacing nearly 12 ounces of sugary beverages with water lowered MASLD risk by 14.4 percent and by 13.5 percent with low- or non-sugar-sweetened drinks. But if you switched completely from a sugary beverage like soda to a low-sugar beverage like fruit juice, the authors found that this did not help lower a person's risk of developing MASLD.

Exactly which beverages raised risk is hard to say because the authors did not directly state what low- or no-sugar drinks people were drinking. That said, there are lots of sodas that are marketed as being healthier alternatives that are not as good as they claim, according to Danbee Kim, MD, nutrition expert and assistant professor at Rutgers New Jersey Medical School.

“Even beverages marketed as ‘natural’ or ‘health-oriented’ may still fall into this category if they rely on sweeteners instead of sugar,” Kim says. “The key commonality is that they provide sweetness without significant calories. However, they may still affect liver health.”

A FEW THINGS may be going on here. “Low/non-sugar sweetened beverages, although low in calories, may still affect liver health through several pathways,” says Lihe Liu, a graduate student researcher at The First Affiliated Hospital of Soochow University in China and lead study author. “They can alter the gut microbiome, interfere with feelings of fullness, increase preference for sweet foods, and in some cases stimulate insulin secretion.”

When you have something that’s sweet, even if it doesn’t contain much or any sugar, it sets off a chain reaction in your body the same way as if you were to actually have sugar, says Albert Matheny, RD, CSCS, registered dietitian and co-founder of SoHo Strength Lab. “You’re basically tricking your body,” he says. “Your body still reacts as if it has sugar.”

As a result, Matheny says your body produces more insulin (a hormone that helps regulate blood sugar), and encourages the liver to take in more sugar from your bloodstream. This can lead to metabolic disruption.

“Some artificial sweeteners may alter how the body handles glucose and insulin, contributing to insulin resistance, which is a key driver of metabolic dysfunction and fat accumulation in the liver,” says Kim. This process may even mess with your appetite, making you think that you're hungry when you're not.

Another reason has nothing to do with your gut, but with your mind. Kim says we can't discount the possibility that people who drink these low- and non-sugar-sweetened beverages may subconsciously compensate for the “saved” calories by eating more sugary or fatty foods later.

Keep in mind that all this is speculation. The explanation for why and how artificially sweetened beverages are linked to MASLD is still under investigation since Liu says her team didn't look too deep into this.

NOT NECESSARILY. “THEY’RE definitely an improvement over traditional diet sodas,” Matheny says. “That’s not to say that they’re a totally healthy option.”

When given a choice, water reigns supreme. And even then you still don't want to go too overboard.

If you still want to crack open a cranberry fizz prebiotic soda or even an unsweetened iced tea, Matheny says that’s probably fine, too. “In moderation, kind of everything is OK.”

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

Memory chip CEO warns 2027 shortage will be so bad that ‘even money can’t buy goods’

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The CEO of Taiwanese memory maker Team Group says the global memory chip shortage is about to get worse, to the point where “even money can’t buy goods.”

Speaking on the company’s third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 2, CEO Gerry Chen (Chen Ching-wen) said the most severe stretch will run from now through the end of the second quarter of 2027, according to a BigGo Finance report. He sees “no downward price trend whatsoever” next year and doesn’t expect supply and demand to balance until the second half of 2028 at the earliest.

The warning reaches well beyond memory makers. The DRAM and NAND flash chips that go into AI servers also go into PCs, smartphones, and cars, and AI buyers are winning the bidding war. That leaves device makers choosing among higher prices, thinner margins, and fewer units.

Chen said AI servers and enterprise applications are taking most DRAM output. The industry’s biggest suppliers, Samsung, SK Hynix, and Micron, have shifted capacity toward the high-bandwidth memory used in AI chips, leaving less conventional memory for consumer devices, as Fortune reported earlier this year.

The money behind that demand is enormous. A recent Stateline analysis called data centers “the largest financial bet in US history.”

The shift shows up in Team Group’s own business. Business-to-business sales made up 64.6% of revenue in the first three quarters of 2026, up from 24.6% a year earlier. Chen attributed the change to market demand rather than a strategic decision by the company.

Contract prices for a 16-gigabit DDR5 chip rose from $8.70 in October 2025 to $48 in September 2026, according to figures cited on the call. That is more than five times the price of a year earlier.

For Team Group, the squeeze has been a windfall. Third-quarter revenue hit a record NT$10.14 billion (about $312 million), up nearly 34% from the prior quarter. Net profit reached NT$3.62 billion (about $111 million), and gross margin climbed to 49.3% from 42.8% in the second quarter.

Device makers sit on the other side of that trade. Apple has reportedly cut component orders for its iPhone 18 Pro models after raising their starting prices by $100, and IDC expects global smartphone shipments to fall 16.7% this year. Consumers are likely to keep absorbing the cost, through pricier PCs and phones or through machines that ship with less memory at the same price.

The most striking part of Chen’s warning is that money and supplier relationships may not be enough. “We have contracts but can’t get enough volume, so we have to go to the spot market and pay a premium to grab supply,” he said, according to Tom’s Hardware. He put the gap between spot and contract prices at 30% to 50%.

New capacity will arrive slowly. Team Group’s new plant in New Taipei City is slated to begin production in the second quarter of 2027. SK Hynix said last month that the shortage will last until 2030, with long-term agreements already covering more than half of its capacity for 2027 and 2028. Harvard Business School professor Willy Shih expects new fabs to reach volume production only in late 2027 or 2028, and has warned that memory booms have always ended once that supply arrives.

The investigation into Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang adds a new variable. Nvidia’s board said on Nov. 8 that it would open an independent probe into Huang’s affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, who is suspected of spying for China and is no relation. President Trump has since said the FBI is “looking into” the matter.

So far, the scandal has not directly touched memory supply. Nvidia, No. 16 on the 2026 Fortune 500, relies on Samsung and SK Hynix for the advanced memory in its AI accelerators, so the exposure runs through export policy. If Washington responds by tightening AI chip controls on China, Korean memory makers could face stricter end-user checks that raise compliance costs and delay shipments, the Korea Herald reported.

Even then, the effect on the broader shortage may be limited. South Korea’s government expects deliveries under existing contracts to continue without disruption, and Hanwha Investment & Securities analyst Park Se-yeon cautioned against tying Huang’s personal controversy directly to Korean chipmakers’ outlook, according to Korea JoongAng Daily.

The next signals arrive quickly: Nvidia reports third-quarter earnings on Nov. 17, and three House committee chairs have requested a closed-door briefing on the case for Nov. 18. For memory buyers, though, Chen’s outlook rests on a simpler problem. “How can prices fall when there’s nothing left?” he said.

Grand Theft Auto 6 becomes the most pre-ordered game in PlayStation Store history

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sony has just announced that Grand Theft Auto 6 has become "the most pre-ordered game in the history of PlayStation Store". Note, it's not the most pre-ordered game in PlayStation history, just on its digital PlayStation Store.

The announcement, which comes as Sony prepares to ditch physical releases for an all-digital future, was made in a PlayStation Blog post confirming pre-loads for GTA 6 are now live.

"Grand Theft Auto 6 is now the most pre-ordered game in the history of PlayStation Store," Sony wrote. "Given this unprecedented demand, we've joined with Rockstar Games to allow players to start pre-loading the game nine days early, so they're ready to play on launch day.

"We strongly advise that everyone who is hoping to play on launch day to pre-load the game as soon as possible," the blog post added. So: consider yourselves advised.

GTA 6 becomes available as soon as your region's clock ticks over from midnight into 19th November, which, if you're in New Zealand, means you'll play the game before everyone else. If only there were a way to pretend you lived there...

Elsewhere in the blog post, Sony underlined some features coming specially to GTA 6 on PS5 and PS5 Pro. On Xbox, meanwhile, cloud streaming is supported, but not - sadly - to PC.

Last night, in a Take-Two financial earnings call, company leader Strauss Zelnick said pre-orders for GTA 6 are tracking "far above expectations" and that soon, "numbers will speak for themselves". Presumably because they will be gripped by an excited form of sentience. ‘Victorian Psycho’ Review: Maika Monroe Scares Up a Very Different Kind of Final Girl

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

[Editor’s note: This review was originally published on May 21, 2026 during the 2026 Cannes Film Festival. Bleecker Street will release the film in theaters Friday, November 13.]

Winifred Notty is the sanest person she knows, or so the heroine (or anti-heroine, depending on how you might see it) of Zachary Wigon’s “Victorian Psycho” is quick to tell anyone who will listen. Or not, as star Maika Monroe delivers that damning and darkly funny insight into Winifred’s soul via a prim voiceover that seems to exist for her own benefit as much as the audience she seems to imagine.

That’s tangled enough, but there’s also this: Winifred doesn’t really know that many people, mostly because just about everyone she’s come across in her life is dead. Still, sane! Ish. Well, it’s not called “Victorian Psycho” for nothing. Related Stories ‘Steps’ Review: A Disposable Fairy Tale Reminds Us That Cinderella Is Someone’s Hated Stepsister Too ‘The Debut’ Trailer: Julianne Moore Goes Full Julianne Moore in Jesse Eisenberg’s Riotously Funny Musical Theater Comedy

Adapted for the screen by novelist Virginia Feito from her own work, the book comes recommended alongside its predecessor, “Mrs. March,” for anyone drawn to entertainment with “psycho” in the title.Beloved scream queen Maika Monroe (“It Follows,” “The Guest,” “Longlegs”) may be switching sides here, offering a twist on who horror fans root for once the blood starts flowing. (Hint: it’s the psycho.)

When we first meet Winifred, the Victorian-era governess is bound for her next assignment to teach the children of Mr. and Mrs. Pounds (Jason Isaacs and a sneaky-great Ruth Wilson, respectively) at their sprawling Yorkshire estate. “This won’t be like the other times,” she tells herself, winking mightily at how all those other times turned out. Our Winifred sports dead eyes and a rictus grin she works so very hard to fix in place. It’s even better when she attempts to imitate those around her, all the better to approximate “being human.”

No question, there’s something very wrong with Winifred. But what? You’ll find out soon enough. Possessed of “a darkness” she cannot shake, Monroe’s Winifred practically vibrates with the need to hold things together. It does not always work for her, but it is nearly always funny, deliciously and dementedly so.

Wigon and Freito don’t balk at the “psycho” promise of their story, as Winifred happily whacks away (often literally) at everyone icky, from gardeners to nurses (Thomasin McKenzie) and, yes, at least one baby. Killing scenes are elegantly, amusingly cut by editors Dustin Chow and Lance Edmands and designed for maximum gasps. Blood flows, then spurts, then pools. Sometimes it gets on Winifred’s face or her collar. She moves on, grimly and happily.

Cinematographer Nico Aguilar brings a similar energy to his work, shooting in and through keyholes, swinging his camera like a clock pendulum, even briefly adapting the perspective of a swallowed (redacted, for spoilers) as it travels into Winifred’s belly. It’s playful and gross, wholly fitting the material. Mostly, it’s so dark and fucked up you can’t help but laugh. OH! Did they do that? Yes, yes they did.

Possessed by the notion that she’s meant to be a part of the Pounds family (who would want to be? these aristocrats are monstrous), Winifred continues to fray. Bloodlusty audiences will be charmed by her go-for-broke attitude (more like go-for-the-throat), plus a steady stream of visual cues as to her current state. Dreams and nightmares merge, she’s haunted by the Pounds’ family mascot of a wild boar (particularly in an ever-morphing tapestry), and she simply can’t stop sneaking into places to stare at people while they sleep.

Clocking in at a tight 90 minutes, some changes to the also-slim book are smart, while others dilute its darkest impulses. Gone is the countdown to a Christmas massacre, which frames the novel; hell, gone is the whole Christmas massacre. Freito’s script leads up to a different sort of celebration, doing away with many of the most colorful supporting characters in the process (Mrs. Fancey, we hardly knew ye!).

That choice isn’t disastrous, although the execution of the big! final! event! is oddly watered down. Here it’s imagined as a battle of Winifred’s wills… but, erm, what wills? The book and the film each offer enough reasons for why our heroine is the way she is, Wigon’s film oddly bisects her spirit by implying she’s not fully on board with all the evil she commits.

For fans of the novel and its wonderfully unrepentant star, this choice might rankle; it’s also odd that Freito would go this route with her own adaptation. “Well, that’s not my ‘Victorian Psycho’ — and she’s not. That’s such a shame because for much of the film, she’s even better. By tempering her impulses and inner monologue, the film ends with less of the giddy gore that makes the rest of it such a dark treat. Dare we say it? We would have loved for this one to go full, ahem, boar.

“Victorian Psycho” premiered at the 2026 Cannes Film Festival. Bleecker Street will release it in theaters on Friday, September 25.

Christopher Meloni Officially Returns to ‘Law & Order: SVU’ This Week for Episode 600

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Hannah covers horror, sci-fi, and games at Collider, where she writes news and features, reviews new releases, and interviews the people who make them. She’s helping build out Collider’s gaming coverage, with Pokémon as one of her specialties. If she isn’t writing about a monster, she’s probably watching one on a tape from her VHS collection.

The last time Olivia Benson and Elliot Stabler saw each other on Law & Order: Special Victims Unit, they were saying goodbye to Captain Donald Cragen. Then Stabler tossed out a casual “Love you” as he left, giving fans something else to talk about after the wake. That was the Season 27 premiere, back in September 2025. This week, Benson has a much harder conversation waiting for him.

Benson and Stabler spent the first 12 seasons of SVU working together. Back then, both of them answered to Cragen. Now Benson is the captain, and she’s the one telling her former partner that they need to take another look at a conviction from the beginning of their careers. In NBC’s promo, she’s already past wondering whether they made a mistake. “This is one of our first cases, and we got it wrong,” she tells him.

Christopher Meloni returns as Elliot Stabler in the 600th episode of Law & Order: SVU, airing Thursday, November 12, at 9 p.m. ET on NBC. Titled “Obedience,” the Season 28 episode reunites Meloni with Mariska Hargitay as the squad follows new leads in one of Benson’s earliest cases. It’s also Meloni’s first appearance on SVU since Law & Order: Organized Crime was canceled after five seasons.

The Rookie started as a show about a man starting over at forty-five and turned into a proper ensemble drama somewhere around season three. Here is which of them you are, and the case for yours being the best of the four.

You started late and you are not embarrassed about it. Nolan walks into the academy at forty-five surrounded by people half his age, and the show is honest that this is both his biggest disadvantage and the only reason he is any good at the job.

You are better at this than people expect and you intend to keep proving it. Chen goes from rookie to the best undercover officer in the division, which works because she is genuinely good at becoming somebody else and genuinely bad at pretending she does not care.

You are hard on people because you have decided that is what caring looks like. Bradford is introduced as the worst training officer in the building and slowly revealed as the one who takes the job most seriously, which are the same fact seen from two angles.

You said what you wanted and then you went and got it. Lopez wanted detective from the first episode, said so out loud, and got there by being better at the work than the people who were quieter about wanting it.

An old conviction gives Benson a reason to call the one person who knows what happened in the room when those early decisions were made. The trouble is that Stabler helped make them. It’s a good use of the reunion because the trust they built as partners cuts both ways. Benson knows she can ask him to come back and help. She also has to ask him to question conclusions they once trusted each other to reach. Meanwhile, it’s her squad that has to follow the new evidence wherever it goes. For the detectives following those leads, that means putting their own captain’s work under the microscope.

The episode also has a second reunion tucked into it. Laura Innes, who played Dr. Kerry Weaver on ER, guest stars opposite Hargitay, who had a recurring role on the medical drama before SVU. William Sadler also guest stars, marking his second appearance on the series after 2019’s “Facing Demons.”

Law & Order: SVU’s 600th episode airs Thursday, November 12, at 9 p.m. ET on NBC and streams Friday, November 13, on Peacock.

I Asked Designers for Their Secrets to an Expensive-Looking Guest Bedroom. They All Said the Same Thing

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Skip the fussy pillow pile and focus on the details guests will actually notice.

Pictured above: a bold aubergine double guest bedroom designed by Nicole Fisher.

The holiday hosting season is upon us, and while you’re planning your tablescape decor and seasonal trimmings, you might want to take a hard look at your guest bedroom.

“Most guest rooms get treated like overflow storage, and guests feel that the second they walk in,” says Marie Cloud, principal designer at Indigo Pruitt.

Even if you host only occasionally, your guest room should give visitors a comfortable place to unwind. No need for a guest book or fuzzy slippers: Designers say these four thoughtful touches can make the room feel luxe without costing a fortune.

Regardless of the design aesthetic, the ambiance of a room always hinges on lighting. “I always pay attention to the lighting,” says Louis Lin, founder of his eponymous New York City–based interior design studio. “A beautiful bedside lamp with a warm, flattering glow can transform the atmosphere of a room. Layering different light sources, rather than relying on overhead lighting, can do that. It's a relatively simple gesture that makes a room feel much more intimate and refined.” This also gives guests the freedom to tailor the air of their space to their liking. It’s one of the few details they can truly control in their temporary lodging.

While a pile of pillows might look inviting, designer Marie Cloud says the bedtime legwork promptly cancels out the aesthetics.

“Skip the stack of eight decorative pillows your guest has to relocate before bed,” says Cloud. Her perfect formula for a top-notch guest setup? Two sleeping pillows, plus one lumbar pillow, all set atop a good set of sheets.

“Sheets with real hand-feel, like a crisp percale or washed linen,” Cloud suggests. “Finish with a throw folded at the foot in a color pulled from the room. It looks considered, and it’s actually comfortable at 2 a.m.”

Most guest rooms get the all-white wall treatment in the name of neutrality, but all it really says is that your space lacks intention. Just because this room is (mostly) reserved for guests doesn't mean it has to lack character. In fact, Cloud says you should not hold back.

“Drench the room,” says the designer. “Walls, trim, and ceiling in the same color, ideally something deep and warm. Continuous color reads custom, it makes a small room feel wrapped rather than cramped, and it tells your guest the space was designed for them. A guest room is also the lowest-risk room in the house to be bold, so take the swing.”

The best guest setups make visitors feel anticipated, even when they show up unexpectedly. To achieve the boutique-hotel feel, designers recommend investing in small luxuries that leave a big impact. A few items Cloud and Lin always recommend having on hand are a small luggage rack, real hangers, fresh towels, and a select set of toiletries reserved especially for guests.

“The real luxury is making guests feel that the room has been prepared just for them,” says Lin.

Chelsea are reportedly confident they could secure a deal for Arsenal midfielder Martin Zubimendi during the January transfer window, with the Blues keen to strengthen their midfield options.

According to a report from The Independent, Chelsea are among the clubs monitoring the Spanish international, alongside Real Madrid and Atletico Madrid, as his position at the Emirates Stadium has become less secure.

The 27-year-old initially established himself as a key figure in Mikel Arteta’s midfield after his £60 million arrival from Real Sociedad in the summer of 2025. However, the situation has changed considerably following Arsenal’s decision to bring in Bruno Guimaraes from Newcastle United.

Arteta now has several options available in central midfield, with Declan Rice and Guimaraes becoming his preferred partnership in recent matches. Myles Lewis-Skelly is also competing for a place, which could make it difficult for Zubimendi to secure regular playing time.

Chelsea could look to take advantage of the situation, particularly after struggling to reinforce their midfield during the previous transfer window. The Spaniard would bring considerable technical quality, composure and defensive intelligence to Xabi Alonso’s side.

His ability to dictate possession and protect the back four could make him an excellent addition to the Premier League outfit.

Arsenal are unlikely to sanction his departure without receiving a substantial transfer fee. The Gunners would reportedly want to recover at least the £60m they invested in the midfielder.

Nevertheless, his reduced importance under Arteta could make an exit increasingly realistic, especially if suitable replacements are available.

Chelsea will face competition from both Madrid clubs, but their interest could become concrete in January. Much will depend on Arsenal’s willingness to negotiate and whether Zubimendi considers a fresh challenge elsewhere.

R.J. Mitte’s Final Director Dedicates Film to Late Actor: “I Can’t Believe He’s Not Here to See It”

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"What we can do for R.J. now is to keep our promise. Honor what we set out to do, and why he signed up to make our film in the first place," says Jeffrey Levine, who helmed Mitte's final role in the social impact film 'SNAFU: Situation Normal All F’d Up (working title),' now in post-production.

By Jeffrey Levine, as Told to Jackie Strause

Jeffrey Levine is executive producer of Morph Media, and producer/writer/director on R.J. Mitte’s final movie, SNAFU: Situation Normal All F’d Up (working title).

This is the beginning of a beautiful friendship.

That was how I felt the last time I saw R.J. Mitte.

Several weeks before his death, I drove R.J. to the airport. We had just finished filming for our movie that R.J. was featured in. R.J. was leaving Los Angeles and was heading back home for a quick turnaround to London for a convention. Related Stories Movies Giancarlo Esposito and John Turturro Hated Each Other on 'Do the Right Thing.' Now They Break Character to Cry Mid-Scene News R.J. Mitte's Mother Shares Tribute After His Death: "We Are Lost Without Him"

The ride took about two hours, and when we arrived at LAX, I got out of the car and I hugged him. I said, “I’ll see you soon.” And he said what he always said, “Let me know if you need anything.” That’s just who R.J. was.

My experience with R.J. was over a shorter period of time than many of the people who have been speaking out since his unexpected death. There are a lot of people who knew R.J. better than me. But he was the type of person where it didn’t matter how long or how short you knew him. Once you did, you knew the real him. He wasn’t an actor who just did a scene and left. He was bigger than that, a positive force, and we were just starting our friendship.

I first met R.J. in May of this year, when our executive producer, Bradford Rand, cast him in our movie, SNAFU: Situation Normal All F’d Up (working title). We had our first conversation over Zoom, the three of us, about him playing an AI executive in our social impact film. It began as a smaller part. And from the second Bradford put me on the phone with R.J. and he heard that our mission for the film was to impact the lives of kids, he said, “I’m in.” That was my experience with him from that day on.

We would talk about the character and the story, because R.J. really cared about this movie, and his role evolved as we produced the film. It became very important for me to have an impactful character arc for him. In the story — after criminals exploit the protagonist’s teenage son — R.J.’s character, an AI developer, is asked to help, and he begins to reflect on the far-reaching negative impact of AI. R.J.’s character uses his position for good and, in the end, he faces a pivotal decision on whether or not to walk away from his high-paying executive job to create what I am hoping someone with influence will one day execute: a global AI regulation company.

The film will inform parents about the dangers of social media and AI, but in a commercial and entertaining way. The objective is for parents to be able to start a dialogue with their kids about the far-reaching effects of AI, social media and our changing media landscape. And it’s R.J.’s character who says, “Enough. We have to do something.” It’s such an impactful character, and I just can’t believe he’s not here today to see it.

Our independent film deals with mental health, which isn’t lost on me now. We wrapped filming not too long ago. We’re currently in post-production. We are in talks on distribution and hope to get the film in front of as many people as possible. But as I’m watching assemblies now, it’s emotionally wrenching. And then there’s R.J.’s extensive behind-the-scenes video addressing AI, and I can’t bring myself to watch that right now.

R.J.’s scenes were so good. It’s crazy how his authenticity carries on screen through the character. Pure talent. Being a part of our project, for R.J., was always about the impact. It was never about money or fame. He wanted the character to be as true, real and impactful as he could be. And he delivered in spades.

This film is the first in a slate for our social impact feature division. I couldn’t imagine a better person to carry this torch. I find myself thinking about R.J. and all the things we talked about, and all the things he truly cared about — his mom and his sister and his fiancé and his work with the Mitte Foundation. R.J. was pure good. It’s a level of human being you don’t see very often.

During our two-hour drive to the airport, he spoke about his foundation and what he wanted to do. He truly wanted to make a difference. He felt like he had a deeper purpose. I asked, “How can we help?” And we talked details, and I’m confident that we will. We’ve been working with the Sentinel Foundation, which deals with the exploitation of kids, and its Clinical Director, Dr. Ashley Poklar, who flew out to be on set during filming. The end of our movie will include a QR code that will direct viewers to resources, both for parents to be able to talk to their children, and for teens who may be going through something and need to know where to go for help.

When I would finish directing a scene with R.J., he’d say, “Are you happy, Jeff? Because if you’re happy, I’m happy.” I am so sad that he suffered and that this happened.

What we can do for R.J. now is to keep our promise. Honor what we set out to do, and why he signed up to make our film in the first place. Create awareness, dialogue and impact as many people as we can. Our film is dedicated to R.J., and for anybody who is going through something. We want our message to reach anyone who needs to hear it.

I didn’t know R.J. for a very long time. But the time I knew him, I’ll never forget.

Mauricio Pochettino 'favourite' to be next Man United manager, club adviser shares release clause verdict

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Manchester United are not undeterred at all by the prospect of paying compensation to lure USA manager Mauricio Pochettino to Old Trafford.

That is according to former Everton chief executive Keith Wyness, speaking exclusively to Football Insider, who views the ex-Tottenham boss as a "very achievable" target for the Red Devils.

Reports on Tuesday have suggested that the 54-year-old has a break clause in his new contract with the USA that can be triggered for a fee of £7.44million.

However, Wyness believes that United are likely looking at a total cost of around £10m for the appointment if Pochettino wants to take his backroom team with him.

This comes amid the increasing likelihood that current manager Michael Carrick is axed, with a key meeting in place for Wednesday.

Club officials are set to meet in Monaco to decide the former midfielder's future on Wednesday, with talks already ongoing regarding his potential exit.

Everton’s former chief Wyness – who served as CEO at Goodison Park between 2004 and 2009 and now runs a football consultancy advising elite clubs – believes that Pochettino is now the firm favourite to take over from Carrick at United.

This comes despite the businessman pointing out that Antonio Conte's agent is continuing to push him for the role.

Speaking on the latest edition of Football Insider's Inside Track podcast, Wyness explained that United won't be deterred by Pochettino's compensation package.

"There are break clauses within his contract, and the number that's being floated is around £7m for him to get out of the USA contract. Now that's very achievable for Man United," he said.

"You'd have to add the rest of his backroom team with him, which would lead to a few more, but still it isn't going to frighten United if it was going to be a £10m package.

"There's still talk about other candidates. Eddie Howe is still being mentioned. Antonio Conte's agent has pushed him right to the front as well, in typical Conte form. But I think Pochettino is the favourite right now.

"The rumours are that there are talks going on right now, and then in Monaco with Sir Jim Ratcliffe and his backroom advisors to see what's going to happen."

Football Insider revealed on Monday that Pochettino will be willing to "listen" to United's plans for him to take charge of the team.

The Argentine has been away from Premier League management since 2024, but has never hidden his desire to return to the English game.

Sources say that Pochettino would be open to the idea of taking the reins at Old Trafford, and will consider it if the club move to sound him out.

He has been a long-term target for the United hierarchy for several years now, and they may finally be about to get their man.

Alexander Skarsgard to Narrate Quinn Audio Erotica Original Set in World of ‘Wicker’

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The story won a fan-fiction contest launched by the app and film ahead of the latter's theatrical release.

The Wicker phenomenon is now venturing into audio erotica.

Wicker star Alexander Skarsgard will narrate Felled, the winning entry in a fan-fiction contest inspired by the world of Black Bear’s hit film, for audio erotica app Quinn, it was announced Tuesday.

Last month, ahead of the film’s theatrical release, Quinn partnered with Wicker to launch a fan-fiction contest inviting fans to create original stories set in the world of the film. With hundreds of submissions from around the world, Emma Parkinson’s Felled was selected as the winning entry. Related Stories Movies 'Godzilla Minus Zero' Powers $31M in Historic Box Office Launch Against Subdued 'Cat in the Hat' Movies 'You Can See Everything,' 'Clayface' Fighting for No. 1 in Quiet Halloween Weekend

The story follows a willow tree that forms a deep attachment to a farmer’s daughter before being cut down. When they reunite years later, they discover that their bond has endured.

“I haven’t really experienced this before, where I’ve been part of a project that has taken on a life of its own like this,“ Skarsgard said in a statement. “It’s also been really fun and inspiring to see how creative people are.”

When asked what drew him to collaborate with Quinn, Skarsgard said, “It was quite refreshing to have something sensual and erotic that wasn’t androcentric for a change!”

“Wicker fans have been so passionate about the film since day one, so partnering was a no-brainer,” says Caroline Spiegel, founder and CEO of Quinn. “There were hundreds of incredible, creative stories about mermen, porcelain men, witches and more. Felled stood out, and we knew our users had to hear Alexander narrate it.”

From directors, co-writers and real-life married couple Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer, Black Bear’s Wicker has taken on a life of its own, going viral on social media and becoming an erotic, meme-making phenomenon.

The film, released on Oct. 23 and based on author Ursula Wills-Jones’s short story The Wicker Husband, follows “an ostracized fisherwoman (Olivia Colman) who commissions a husband made of wicker (Skarsgard) from the local basketmaker (Peter Dinklage). When her new husband awakens her desires and captures the village’s attention, jealousy, fascination and chaos follow, challenging the conventions of marriage, sex and the rules that bind them.“

“We always thought of the sex scenes as the comedy of the film, too,“ Wilson told THR. “They were supposed to be played for laughs, and they are. But I think once we actually had Alexander in costume, Olivia and Ita O’Brien, our intimacy coordinator, working together on these scenes — that’s when they did become surprisingly more sexy than we thought they would.“

The film even features a full frontal look but only when the Colman’s fisherwoman is looking at the nude wicker man off in the distance in the water.

Felled is the latest Quinn original series to be added to the company’s growing catalog of produced romances that have enlisted Hollywood talent. Other stars who have lent their voices to Quinn originals include Wilson Bethel, Chris Briney, Andrew Scott, Manny Jacinto, Tom Blyth, Jamie Campbell Bower, Victoria Pedretti, Jesse Williams, Lucien Laviscount, Thomas Doherty, Katherine Moennig, Costa D’Angelo, Tyriq Withers, Shawn Hatosy, Sam Heughan and Matt Smith.

Stars who have teamed for Quinn duet productions include Love Hypothesis stars Tom Bateman and Lili Reinhart (Olympus U), Heated Rivalry stars Hudson Williams and Connor Storrie (Ember & Ice) and Off Campus stars Mika Abdalla and Stephen Kalyn (Rent Free).

Felled by Emma Parkinson, narrated by Skarsgard, is available now on Quinn.

High oil prices have led to a dip in global carbon emissions

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Bucking recent trends, fossil fuel emissions look to be down 0.4 percent.

Evaluating the annual global temperatures each year requires context. There is a fair amount of natural variability from one year to the next, so it’s important to focus on the trend over time. Famously, global warming did not end in 1998, as some claimed a couple of decades ago—we just had a run of La-Niña-dominated years after a very strong El Niño.

Annual greenhouse gas emissions are much less variable, having increased fairly steadily, but many complicating factors can still muddy the picture. Sometimes those factors conspire to throw a year off the trend. It looks like 2026 will be one of those years.

A new analysis led by Corinne Le Quéré at the University of East Anglia estimates that fossil fuel emissions have fallen about 0.4 percent from last year. This probably isn’t a peak that symbolizes heartening progress on climate change, though; the researchers think it’s a short-term dip driven by the war in Iran and its effect on oil prices.

This team works on the Global Carbon Project, which compiles stats on fossil fuel use and things like deforestation to track carbon emissions globally and by country. In this case, they looked at the stats and tried to pull out key underlying trends that show us what to expect over the next couple of years.

Last year, fossil fuel emissions increased 1.2 percent, a bit above the 2019–2025 average of 0.8 percent per year. This year’s decline featured a 1.9 percent drop in oil emissions, a smaller 0.3 percent drop in emissions from natural gas use, and a 0.6 percent increase in coal emissions.

Unlike the pandemic-driven drop in 2020, this wasn’t driven by a widespread decrease in energy demand. Demand did increase, but additions of electric vehicles and solar and wind generation covered almost 70 percent of the growth. Electric vehicles can account for only about 20 percent of that consequential decline in oil emissions, meaning the bulk is due to high prices.

One simple way to break down emissions is to connect it to economic output. Given a country’s gross domestic product (GDP), energy use per unit GDP, and carbon emissions per unit energy, you can calculate total carbon emissions. GDP might go up, but if that’s due to less energy-intensive industries or a cleaner energy system, carbon emissions might not rise with it.

Between 2019 and 2025, 59 countries cut their emissions despite economic growth, even as global emissions increased by 0.8 percent per year. The researchers estimated that, had the economic growth not been accompanied by the growth of electric vehicles and solar and wind generation, the increase would have been much higher, at 1.9 percent per year.

Given the International Monetary Fund’s projection for GDP through 2028, the researchers extrapolated these trends forward as well. From that, we would expect global fossil fuel emissions to continue increasing. That includes the present year—without the shock to oil prices, we would be seeing emissions increase in 2026 as well.

So while we could be close to peak emissions, we’re probably not there yet. The growth of electric vehicles and renewable energy is offsetting most of the growth of emissions, but not all of it. That would take just a bit more juice.

The researchers write, “If further acceleration of clean technology deployment can be supported through time, a sustained peak and decline in global CO2 emissions could be a few years away.”

Nature Climate Change, 2026. DOI: 10.1038/s41558-026-02821-8 (About DOIs).

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Nanobody Shows Promise in Myasthenia Gravis

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

— PREVAIL trial found rapid improvement with a once-weekly subcutaneous injection

Results from the phase III PREVAIL trial presented at the American Association of Neuromuscular & Electrodiagnostic Medicine annual meeting showed that treatment with the investigational once-weekly nanobody gefurulimab significantly improved symptoms in adults with anti-acetylcholine receptor antibody-positive generalized myasthenia gravis, with a safety profile generally comparable to placebo.

In this exclusive MedPage Today video, James Winkley, MD, of Baptist Health Medical Group in Lexington, Kentucky, discusses the findings, including the potential convenience of self-administered treatment with the subcutaneous injectable and compatibility with intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) therapy.

Following is a transcript of his remarks:

The PREVAIL study was a double-blind, placebo-controlled trial with gefurulimab as the treating agent. Gefurulimab is a unique agent in that it's the first nanobody that'll be used in myasthenia gravis, which makes it a smaller molecule, which allows for greater tissue penetration and solubility -- so it can be given as a [subcutaneous] injection rather than the larger monoclonal antibodies, which have to be given in larger volumes underneath the skin or as IV infusion. So this would represent a greater ease of access for patients to have a [subcutaneous], once-a-week injection that doesn't require them to go to the infusion center.

As we had seen in the trial, similar to eculizumab [Bkemv] and ravulizumab [Ultomiris], which are C5 inhibitors, it met its primary outcome of MG-ADL [Myasthenia Gravis Activities of Daily Living] reduction, with a minus 4.2 reduction versus 2.1 on placebo, which was statistically significant. We did see a quick onset of action within 1 week, with a statistically significant reduction in the MG-ADL for those patients, as well as 4 weeks for the [Quantitative Myasthenia Gravis].

There were similar side effects between the treatment group and the placebo group, with injection-site reactions being slightly higher in the treatment group, but all of those reactions tended to be mild to moderate. The most common adverse events were headaches, upper respiratory infections, and muscle aches and pains. One thing that was significant at any time we're using complement inhibitors, there were no meningococcal infections. Everybody made sure they were fully vaccinated, and if they'd had to be started prior to 2 weeks, they received antibiotics. But we had a very typical safety profile, as I said, between the placebo arm and the treatment arm -- the side effects were similar.

This is going to make for ease of access for patients. You still have other molecules out on the market. You've got a once-daily complement inhibitor and you've got now a once-every-2-weeks IV infusion versus every 8 weeks. So kind of depending on what the patient prefers, this gives them greater access. We've got an easily portable complement inhibitor that patients no longer are tethered to an infusion center that they have to worry about. When's our next infusion? Do I have access? And I think that's going to be a huge advantage for the patients and their quality of life.

I would say that one thing that's unique about [gefurulimab] is that it is compatible with IVIG. And so the other larger monoclonal antibodies like the FcRn [neonatal Fc receptor] and the larger eculizumab and ravulizumab, it gets recycled by a different mechanism because it goes through the albumin on the FcRn receptor. It was combined with IVIG during the trial, patients could be on stable dosages of it, so it has that advantage of being able to be compared and not have to give supplemental dosing.

So I think that it will have combination with other available medications. I think that's a big advantage for it.

Candreva e la Lazio: «non ho mai rifiutato» il ruolo di vicecapitano

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Pioli gli propose l’incarico in camera; l’ex giocatore ricorda anche l’accoglienza dei tifosi dopo il gol al San Paolo.

Antonio Candreva non ha mai rifiutato di diventare vicecapitano della Lazio. L’ex biancoceleste lo ha chiarito a Crazy Calcio Show, ripercorrendo anche il rapporto con i tifosi e la notte trascorsa senza dormire prima della finale di Coppa Italia contro la Roma. Oggi fa parte dello staff tecnico della Nazionale Under 15.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Candreva ricorda i quattro anni e mezzo alla Lazio come un’esperienza indimenticabile. L’inizio, però, non fu semplice: pesava su di lui una presunta fede romanista. Il gol segnato al San Paolo contro il Napoli segnò una svolta nel rapporto con i sostenitori biancocelesti.

«Quello è stato il momento in cui sono stato accolto dalla Lazio», ha raccontato. Candreva descrive quell’avvicinamento come qualcosa voluto sia da lui sia dal pubblico, una liberazione. Ha aggiunto che la curva biancoceleste era quella capace di dargli più carica in campo e che ancora oggi è felice di aver ricevuto un coro dai tifosi.

L’ex giocatore ha poi smentito la ricostruzione secondo cui avrebbe rinunciato a fare il vicecapitano quando la fascia passò sul braccio di Biglia. «È una storia falsa, perché non pretendevo nulla», ha precisato. Davanti a lui, ha ricordato, c’erano giocatori come Radu, Marchetti e Klose: per questo non si aspettava alcun incarico.

Quando Pioli lo chiamò in camera per proporgli il ruolo, la questione fu discussa insieme. Candreva ha ribadito il punto con parole nette: «non ho mai rifiutato».

Report: Dolphins DT Zach Sieler to have season-ending biceps surgery

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Dolphins will reportedly be without one of their top defensive players for the rest of the season.

Adam Schefter of ESPN reports that defensive tackle Zach Sieler tore his biceps early in Sunday’s loss to the Lions. He was able to play the rest of the game, but needs to have surgery that will end his season.

Sieler started the first eight games of the season for the Dolphins. He had 24 tackles and two sacks in those appearances. He joined the team during the 2019 season and has 377 tackles, 37.5 sacks, two interceptions, five forced fumbles and six fumble recoveries during his time in Miami.

Sieler signed a three-year extension with the Dolphins ahead of the 2025 season. The deal ties him to the team through the 2029 season with cap hits of more than $20 million each year.
