Scott McTominay 'had shock on heart' before Scotland World Cup game as ex-Man Utd star opens up on summer

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Scotland midfielder Scott McTominay has revealed that he needed to have his heart “shocked” two days before the nation’s World Cup opener against Haiti.

McTominay explained that he was taken to hospital 48 hours before the match on June 14 and was “put to sleep”. At the time, his absence from training was put down to a sickness bug, but the 29-year-old has spoken for the first time on what he went through during the summer.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

McTominay played the full 90 minutes in the 1-0 win over the Haitians and started the next two games against Morocco and Brazil, which Scotland lost to exit the tournament at the group stage.

McTominay then went on holiday before returning to his club side Napoli, who announced on September 1 that the former Man Utd player was to have a procedure to correct a "mild benign arrhythmia". He missed the last international window under new head coach Sebastien Pocognoli, but is now back in the camp for matches against North Macedonia and Switzerland in the coming days.

“I’m good,” said McTominay in his broadcast press conference on Tuesday. “I’m feeling much, much better than I was. I feel like it was something that needed to be done. In your career you have moments like that where things like that can take you by surprise. It looks a lot worse in the media for family and friends - they see news relating to a heart issue - but it’s a relatively straightforward procedure that went really well. I’m really happy to be back and playing football again. That’s the most important thing.

“I feel like it was explained to me really, really well by the doctors at Napoli and my cardiologist in Manchester alongside the doctor in Scotland when we were away at the World Cup. He explained that if this was the case, this is how it would look. Credit to all the staff members, they made me so relaxed about the situation.”

Explaining how the World Cup unfolded, McTominay continued: “The World Cup, it was difficult for me, to be honest. I had to go and get the shock on my heart. That was two days before the Haiti game. You prepare so well, I was in such good condition and something like that happens. You’re thinking ‘how has this happened to me’. But then you have to get on with it. That’s the cards you are dealt in football. Sometimes things don’t go your way and you have to continue to do your best. It’s nowhere near an excuse but it’s something you have to look back on and say ‘well, it’s part of the process.’

“Obviously the way the World Cup went, there were at least two, three weeks of me sitting there disappointed, not quite there mentally. I needed time to settle down and have a break.

“I was 100 per cent [fitness wise], but whenever you are sat in a hospital bed two days before the World Cup, about to get put to sleep, it’s not ideal, is it? So whenever things like that happen ... it is what it is, there’s nothing you can do about it. After the World Cup, I reflected on it and I was extremely disappointed. Probably the most disappointed I’ve ever been in my career.

“After the World Cup, my summer holidays weren’t heavily centred around thinking about the World Cup, but it plays a part. Whenever you are disappointed or angry about something, you go away on your holidays and it’s just not the same. You are sat there thinking ‘we could have done more’ but that three-week break I had with my operation done me the world of good.

“I’m always one to be glass half-full but after the World Cup I was like ‘this is a tough one to take’. But you have to refresh and you have to come out stronger at the other side.”

Ben White: England legend demands 'lifetime ban' for Arsenal star after squad snub

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Chris Waddle has called for a “lifetime ban” for Ben White after the Arsenal defender’s previous refusal to represent England.

White ended a self-imposed exile from the England national set-up in March 2026 when he accepted a call-up from boss Thomas Tuchel, featuring in friendlies against Japan and Uruguay.

Before that, White had not represented his country since the 2022 World Cup, quitting the tournament shortly before the end of the group stage for ‘personal reasons’.

It has been suggested that White’s refusal to play for England stemmed from a ‘barbed comment’ made by then-assistant manager Steve Holland, with former Three Lions manager Gareth Southgate reiterating that he would call-up the Arsenal defender if he was available for selection.

Waddle, capped 62 times by his country, believes that White should never have been given the chance to play for England again.

Speaking to iGaming.com, Waddle stated: “Listen, Ben White shouldn’t be in the England squad, that’s my opinion.

“He refused to represent his country. I'm surprised Thomas (Tuchel) brought him back in, and I don't get why he has. There might be a shortage of fullbacks, but when somebody turns down playing for their country, like Ben White did at Euro 2024, to me that's a lifetime ban.

“I'd say, ‘Forget it mate, on my watch you're not getting back in.’”

The former Tottenham midfielder said he would go one step further, permanently marking White’s England record after his refusal to accept a call-up.

Waddle added: “Once you turn your country down, there should be an asterisk next to your name that means you will never get the opportunity to put on that England shirt again. End of story.”

White has been included in the Three Lions’ squad to face Croatia and Spain in the Nations League, prompting discourse about his refusal to play for England under Gareth Southgate.

Despite Waddle’s strong comments, White appears to have the support of current England internationals.

Jordan Henderson in March stated that Three Lions internationals ‘would always support’ White, while manager Tuchel has described his decision to recall the defender to the fold as a ‘new chapter’.

Love Island’s Trinity Reveals Living Situation With Bryce in L.A.

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Trinity Tatum exclusively told E! News at the 2026 EBONY Power 100 what her and Bryce Alakai’s life together in L.A. looks like after their Love Island USA win: “I make him come over all the time.”

Trinity Tatum and Bryce Alakai’s romance was never meant to stay in Fiji.

In fact, Tatum recently moved from her native Virginia to Los Angeles, where Alakai is based, following their win on Love Island USA season eight—and their new chapter is going swimmingly.

“I just got my apartment,” she exclusively told E! News in a joint interview with fellow islander Aniya Harvey at the 2026 EBONY Power 100 event on Nov. 9. “But it’s so funny because I literally just spent the night over Bryce’s house last night, so it’s like do a combination of I go over his house, he comes over mine.”

For them, having that balance in their relationship is crucial.

“He’s like, ‘I’m paying rent. I still have to go home,’” the 23-year-old quipped. “I make him come over all the time and he’s like, ‘Girl, I’m still paying rent to be here. L.A. rent is not cheap, honey.’”

That’s why Tatum and Alakai, 30, have “been mixing and dabbling” to get a "mix of both.” And the life they’ve built is exactly her type on paper.

“I’ve been in L.A. a lot,” she continued. “I’ve been around my man. I’ve been around all his friends and all of my girls.”

As for their holiday plans, the two will be making a splash with their loved ones.

“I am spending the holidays in Boston,” Tatum shared. “I want to spend a lot of it with my mom. We’re both spending it with both of our families.”

And Harvey is keen to do the same. After all, she revealed her plans to throw a huge holiday party in her native Atlanta, sharing she’ll be inviting “every single person.”

And that of course includes the rest of the core four ladies, including Kayda Bosse and Melanie Moreno, who Tatum shared she still talks to daily.

And Harvey, 24, makes it a point to stay connected as well. As she put it, “I feel like we do a good job of being in tune with one another.”

But in addition to finding both love and friendship in the Villa, the core four have also found success, a fact that Tatum shared they still “process” every day.

“I feel like every time we go to an event like this or anything we’ve been invited to, we are processing it,” she said. “Like, wow we’re really here.”

And Harvey echoed that sentiment, adding, “We take it in every single day. It’s such a blessing.”

The ultimate Love Island success story, Nathan and Cara were declared season two's winning pair after meeting in the villa in 2016.

The duo exchanged vows in June 2019 and welcomed their second child, daughter Delilah, in July 2020.

U.S. gas prices fall below $4 for the first time since July

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Americans may finally find some relief at the pump.

The national average price of regular gasoline fell below $4 a gallon for the first time since July, reaching $3.9788 on Tuesday, according to AAA. Prices are down about 17 cents from a week ago and nearly 39 cents from a month ago.

The decline comes after energy inflation slowed significantly in October. Energy prices increased 0.7% during the month, a far cry from the 5.2% surge seen in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. The BLS' gasoline index increased 1% over the month, after advancing 9.1% in September.

Bespoke Investment Group pointed out that gasoline prices also tend to decline at this time of the year, making part of the recent relief seasonal.

"While lower oil prices have been a tailwind for the relief at the pump, it's also perfectly normal for gas prices to be falling at this time of year, so if they weren't, there would really be something wrong," the firm said.

The drop at the pump followed a sharp retreat in crude oil. International oil benchmark Brent crude on Tuesday briefly fell below $90 a barrel for the first time since August as comments from U.S. President Donald Trump fueled hopes for an end to Washington's nearly nine-month conflict with Iran.

Recovering oil flows from the Persian Gulf have also eased supply concerns. Regional crude exports have returned to an estimated 80% to 85% of prewar levels, including pipeline shipments, according to ING.

"Energy markets remain under pressure as hopes for a de-escalation in the Middle East reduce geopolitical risk premiums. Oil extended its losses as Gulf supply flows continued to recover, while European gas also moved lower," analysts at ING wrote.

However, the Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller warned Tuesday that energy shocks may have more persistent effects on inflation than they did before the pandemic.

"While I am pleased to see energy prices down from their high rates over the past week or so, they are still quite elevated relative to the start of the year, and the ultimate effect on inflation will depend on what levels are sustained," he said.

Here's the inflation breakdown for October 2026 — in one chart

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Inflation throttled back in October from its blistering pace in September amid a more moderate rise in gasoline prices, but remained high as categories like food and housing put upward pressure on consumer prices.

The consumer price index, an inflation barometer, rose 0.4% on a monthly basis in October, after adjusting for seasonal factors, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday. That's down from 0.6% in September but higher than the 0.3% that economists expected.

"It's uncomfortably high," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's. "The Fed's not going to be happy about it. It certainly makes it more likely we get another rate increase in December."

The Federal Reserve uses inflation data to guide its interest rate policy. The U.S. central bank raised rates in September amid a recent pickup in inflation, which economists largely attribute to the war in Iran. It held rates steady at its last meeting in October.

Economists generally assess a measure known as "core" CPI — which strips out volatile food and energy prices — to examine underlying inflation dynamics in the U.S. economy.

That core metric rose 0.3% in October, as it also did in September and August. A consistent monthly reading around 0.2% would be more in line with the Fed's 2% annual inflation target, Zandi said.

Inflation has been above the Fed's target for more than five years.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics didn't report an annual inflation figure in the latest CPI report because of the lengthy federal government shutdown that happened a year ago. That shutdown, which ran from Oct. 1 to Nov. 12, disrupted the agency's typical data collection, and it didn't release an inflation report for October 2025.

"Investors do not need year-over-year figures to see that this was a hot inflation report and that prices are not on a sustainable path back toward the Fed's 2% target," Jason Pride, chief of investment strategy and research at Glenmede, a wealth management firm, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

"It would have taken exceptional evidence of disinflation to dissuade the Fed from raising rates in December, and this report delivered the opposite," he wrote.

Economists pointed to four big reasons for the hotter-than-expected CPI reading.

Perhaps the largest contributor to the outsized inflation reading in Tuesday's CPI report was housing, Zandi said.

Housing is the largest component of CPI, and movements in the category strongly influence the index's month-to-month reading, he said.

The "shelter" CPI index rose 0.3% in October, accounting for over 30% of the CPI increase, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Zandi said he doesn't expect that dynamic to persist, however, because it's not consistent with the trajectory of market rents in the U.S.

"It does feel like we have a high level of monthly volatility" in the CPI data, he said. "It's all over the map month to month."

But economists said broader factors are putting upward pressure on prices, too.

"Core inflation today reflects three outsized shocks: tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related demand," Josh Hirt, senior U.S. economist at Vanguard Group, an asset manager, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

The Iran war has raised oil prices, pushing up prices for fuels like gasoline, diesel and jet fuel, which are refined from crude oil, economists said.

Gasoline prices rose 1% in October, a significant pullback after a 9.1% monthly increase in September, CPI data showed.

While diesel prices have retreated from recent record highs, they remain elevated and could spill more broadly into the U.S. economy in the coming months, economists said.

"Diesel prices have a really slow pass-through to all the areas of CPI, given it affects transportation costs," said Thomas Ryan, a senior North America economist at Capital Economics.

"These things take time," Ryan said. "It'll be an inflationary impetus for at least the next three to six months, I suspect."

There was some evidence of that broader percolation of diesel prices in October, such as in food prices, Zandi said. Higher diesel prices add to the cost of transporting food to the grocery store shelves, he said.

Food prices rose 0.5% in October after an unchanged reading in September, according to CPI data.

The run-up in jet fuel costs has also led airlines to raise their ticket prices, economists said. Airfares climbed 0.4% in October, after monthly increases of 1.2%, 2.7% and 3.8% in the preceding months, respectively, according to the CPI report.

Meanwhile, the inflationary impact of tariffs imposed by the Trump administration should fade by the end of the year, assuming the White House doesn't renew its push for more import taxes, Zandi said.

Certain categories of physical goods, like toys, furniture, and appliances, saw elevated inflation rates in October, likely because businesses have passed on tariff costs to consumers, he said.

The buildout of data centers that power artificial intelligence has driven up prices for electricity and consumer electronics, economists said.

Those data centers have led to a shortage of technology equipment, such as memory chips used in household electronic products like iPhones, Ryan said.

Police 'are trawling through dodgy Fire Stick sellers' bank accounts to contact illegal viewers' of Premier League football and films

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A former detective has revealed the lengths authorities are going to in order to crack down on the use and sale of 'dodgy Fire Sticks'.

Law enforcement organisations across the UK are taking a tougher stance on these devices – and how they are used and distributed – with multiple people having been convicted and jailed in recent years for selling and supplying them.

The devices, which often cost anywhere between £30 and £100, give customers access to a range of premium TV services without paying, most notably in a football sense with access to live matches.

But Rav Wilding, a former detective who recently appeared on the BBC Morning Live show, has sent a fresh warning to individuals that use them.

Wilding said: 'People are buying them online usually on the black market and then they're getting all these services for free essentially.

'But it's a crime. It's not allowed. Owning the stick itself is not a crime, but using it for this purpose is.'

A former detective has revealed the lengths authorities are going to in order to crack down on the use and sale of 'dodgy Fire Sticks'

Wilding went on to say that along with a crackdown on sellers through house raids, authorities are now looking through their accounts to find out in further detail who is using the devices.

They then often contact those users in a new step, with it estimated that around four million people or households have got these Fire Sticks at the moment.

Wilding added: 'All of them are using them illegally. They're going for the distributors. They're going for the sellers. They are having a lot of success. But actually, once they've got the seller, they're going through their accounts.

'They're seeing who has been paying the money for these illegal things, and if they've been paying them for these sticks then people should possibly expect a conversation or two if the police want to know why they've been paying them money for this.'

Wilding revealed that this included checking bank statements to find out the information of individuals who have been paying for such a service, and authorities have been joined by subscription service providers in their clampdown attempts too.

'They (subscription service providers) are losing money to this, so they have been reaching out and are taking action against those people using them illegally as well,' he explained.

'So I wouldn't say it's something you should do. It is actually a crime and it can have a nasty fine, a huge fine and also a custodial sentence for users and sellers of these things.'

Rav Wilding appeared on BBC Morning Live and sent a fresh warning to users of the devices

Daily Mail Sport has previously reported FACT is playing a leading role in the crackdown on the devices, with the anti-piracy organisation liaising with police forces across the country.

And Kieron Sharp, chairman of FACT, added: 'We have a wide range of enforcement options - from issuing cease and desist notices to "knock and talk" visits - through to full criminal prosecutions where appropriate.

'We will take robust action against those involved - not only targeting sellers and distributors, but also those facilitating and profiting from the illegal activity.'

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/finance/hims-hers-reports-8904m-q3-revenue-tightens-2026-outlook" hreflang="en">Hims &amp; Hers reports $890.4M Q3 revenue, tightens 2026 outlook</a>

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Hims & Hers reports $890.4M Q3 revenue, tightens 2026 outlook" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Digital health and wellness company Hims & Hers tightened its full-year 2026 revenue outlook after missing Wall Street analysts’ revenue and earnings estimates.

Andrew Dudum, co-founder, chairman and CEO, pointed investors toward the increasing scale of Hims & Hers' pharmacy and lab infrastructure. That growth is driving efficiencies for the company, as is its artificial intelligence native experience, he said.

“That infrastructure positions us to launch treatments of increasing complexity, including testosterone replacement therapy this quarter and our Peptides Plus offering later this month,” Dudum said.

Peptides have been a focus for the company as of late, with Dudum telling investors last quarter it plans to offer already-permitted peptides, like sermorelin and NAD+.

Dudum said Hims & Hers’ long-term strategy focuses on “three critical commitments:” being a leader in metabolic health domestically and globally, leading major markets globally and being "at the forefront of category expansion.”

“Each of these reinforces the other, and together they describe how Hims & Hers becomes the global destination for consumer health,” he said. “We are investing aggressively against all three, because this opportunity is growing quickly, and we believe it will become more valuable every quarter.”

The company reported $890.4 million in revenue in Q3, up 49% compared to revenue of $599 million the year prior, according to financial results published Monday. Net loss was $33 million, compared to $15.8 million in profit the same quarter a year ago.

Chief Financial Officer Yemi Okupe said revenue excluded $18 million from the “scaling of membership revenue” in the company’s weight loss offering, but will be recognized in the fourth quarter.

“This revenue deferral has no impact on free cash flow generation,” Okupe noted.

Adjusted EBITDA was $82.4 million compared to $78.4 million the year prior.

Ethan Feller, stock strategist at Zacks Investment Research, told Fierce Healthcare the company’s revenue was “roughly what Wall Street expected,” adding that U.S. “growth sped up to 21%.”

“But the company lost 14 cents a share against an expected gain of 6 cents, its third big miss in a row,” Feller said. “It is also keeping less of each sale, as gross margin fell to 57%, down from 74% a year ago, as more of the business shifts to lower-margin brand-name weight-loss drugs.”

Feller added the company’s “growth story is still a real long-term tailwind.”

“Demand for weight-loss drugs is strong, and having both Novo and Lilly on the platform helps,” Feller said. “But as long as margins keep shrinking, the stock will likely stay under pressure, until either the price falls to a level that reflects the lower margins or the company shows it can turn a steady profit.”

As a result, Hims & Hers tightened its full-year 2026 revenue guidance to a range of $3.13 billion to $3.15 billion and reduced its adjusted EBITDA guidance to a range of $257 million to $277 million.

“This is a reflection of the more aggressive strategic pricing and marketing actions that we expect to take in the U.K. and Germany, and the weight loss membership deferral noted above,” Okupe said.

The earnings report comes on the heels an announcement that the company struck a deal with Eli Lilly to offer its branded obesity treatments directly through the Hims & Hers platform. The drugs were previously only available to patients if they left the site and went to Lilly's direct-to-consumer platform, LillyDirect.

“Nearly 3 million people across four continents rely on us to access care,” Dudum said. “That trust has made us a valuable partner to global pharmaceutical leaders like Novo and Lilly, and positions us to keep bringing the most innovative treatments to our customers as they come to market.”

Dudum added the company is planning to launch “flexible multi-month subscriptions” across its assortment to make it easier for customers to address health goals.

“We will continue to prioritize partnerships across the industry, with the goal that whenever an innovative therapy reaches the market, our customers can access it here,” Dudum said. “That assortment will sit alongside an AI-native experience that will soon be available to nearly every weight loss customer on our platform.”

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/digital-health/oura-acquire-ai-virtual-primary-care-partner-counsel-health-625m" hreflang="en">Oura to acquire AI virtual primary care partner Counsel Health for $625M</a>

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Oura to acquire AI virtual primary care partner Counsel Health for $625M" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Smart ring maker and health tracking company Oura plans to acquire AI virtual primary care company and business partner Counsel Health for $625 million, the health tech companies announced Tuesday.

The all-cash deal is expected to close before the end of this year, pending customary closing conditions. Bringing Counsel’s platform and capabilities in-house will allow more Oura members to bring their biometric data into a virtual, in-app care encounter, the companies said in their announcement.

“Oura helps members understand their physiology and, with Counsel, they can take action on their health across a range of needs, from quick questions and prescriptions to lab results, chronic care, and referrals,” Tom Hale, CEO of Oura, said. “Together, we can create a new model of care that extends the current healthcare paradigm by delivering an experience that’s more immediate, personalized, and proactive. This is a massive step in our mission to empower people to live healthier, longer, by delivering on the promise of a 24-7 doctor in your pocket.”

Counsel’s model uses text-based, records-informed medical AI encounters to help field and triage users, providing a path to telehealth encounters with the company’s in-house physicians, who are also supported on the backend by a proprietary “Clinician Cockpit” that surfaces relevant clinical data for the encounter. The two-year-old company boasts enterprise partnerships with health plans, provider organizations, employers and other consumer health organizations, for whom it says its approach can reduce costs and improve access to timely care.

Oura is a digital health giant with 5.7 million paid members, whose recently postponed IPO was targeting a $15.6 billion valuation. The company says it is profitable and “growing meaningfully” with 92% year-over-year revenue growth (nearly $1.8 billion) for the fiscal year ended Sept. 30, but ultimately delayed the offering due to “uncertainty” in the market. Its most recent fiscal quarter, ended Sept. 30, included 153% year-over-year revenue growth an adjusted EBITDA of $87 million.

Oura has partnered with Counsel since June, and in September announced a tighter collaboration centered on the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services’ ACCESS (Advancing Chronic Care with Effective Scalable Solutions) Model, set to begin early next year. The companies have said early data from their partnership show strong care satisfaction among those who saw a physician (4.6 out of 5 average rating), widespread agreement among Counsel members (90%) that use of their Oura data helped improve care, and general Oura member satisfaction (70%) with the onboarding to Counsel’s services.

“At Counsel, we’ve built medical AI around trust, safety and responsibility, keeping physicians in the loop for clinical decisions,” Muthu Alagappan, M.D., founder and CEO of Counsel, said in the announcement. “Oura’s data gives clinicians objective context, trended biometrics, and member engagement to deliver more informed care. From the start, our teams bonded over a joint vision, shared culture, and mission, further solidifying as we negotiated our deal over the past few months.”

Under a new owner, the company will continue to serve its existing enterprise partners while expanding to new customers and broadening its clinical capacity. For the latter, Oura and Counsel said they plan to bolster preventive care via continuous monitoring and proactive guidance; acute care via timely intervention for episodic health issues; and specialty and chronic care management in areas like reproductive health, cardiometabolic health and aging.

“Oura tells members what’s happening in their bodies, and Counsel helps them act on it,” summarized Oura Executive Chairman David Shuman. “This combination expands our addressable market and deepens the value we deliver to every member. This not only increases lifetime value, but Counsel’s enterprise partners also open the door to millions of people beyond our existing member base.”

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/positive-medicaid-signals-open-enrollment-trends-recapping-major-payers-q3-results" hreflang="en">Positive Medicaid signals, open enrollment trends: Recapping major payers' Q3 results</a>

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Positive Medicaid signals, open enrollment trends: Recapping major payers' Q3 results" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

While medical cost trend remains a pressure point for health insurers, leading companies cited some positive trends, too, heading into the final months of 2026.

UnitedHealth Group was once again the most profitable company among payers in the quarter, bringing in $4.1 billion in profit for Q3. That's up from a $2.3 billion haul in the prior-year quarter.

Through the first nine months of the year, profits were $15.9 billion at UHG, rising from $12 billion a year ago.

The healthcare giant has been in the midst of a critical financial turnaround since mid-2025, and in the most recent quarter, it highlighted some of the progress that it's logged over the past year at one of it's biggest pain centers: Optum Health.

When the company posted an uncharacteristically poor quarter to start 2025, Optum Health was cited as one of the main pressure points. During its most recent call, Optum CEO Patrick Conway, M.D., said the transition centered on a "continued return to value-based care principles" that sit at the core of Optum Health.

UnitedHealth also led the way on revenue in Q3, posting $112.7 billion. That's down slightly from $113.2 billion the company reported in the third quarter of 2025.

CVS Health had the second-highest profit and revenue in Q3, bringing in $2.2 billion in earnings for the quarter. This marks a significant reversal from the prior-year quarter, when the company posted a nearly $4 billion loss.

CVS' revenue in the quarter was $108.4 billion, increasing from $102.9 billion in the third quarter of 2025.

The company's financial performance wasn't what dominated the headlines in Q3, however, as CVS shocked the industry in announcing plans to split its pharmacy business off and form two companies. The break-up is expected to close in the first half of 2028, and would establish CVS Pharmacy as its own company while Aetna and Caremark would remain together as CVS Health.

CVS CEO David Joyner said that the shift represents "a structure and plan that are built to win and represent the best path to create value over the long term."

The Cigna Group and Elevance Health were on par with one another in Q3 on profits, both earning $1.4 billion. Cigna wins out on revenue, though, with $73.7 billion, while Elevance reported $51.4 billion.

By comparison, Cigna posted $1.9 billion in profit and $69.7 billion in revenue in Q3 2025.

Core to the third quarter conversation was the company's newly-unveiled Lead to One healthcare transformation strategy, which puts an emphasis on building a personalized experience for its members and customers.

Cigna said it plans to lean on all of the segments under its umbrella to drive toward meeting individual member needs.

Elevance Health, meanwhile, increased its profit slightly year over year from $1.2 billion in Q3 2025. Revenues in the prior-year quarter were $50.7 million.

The company was one of several insurers in the quarter that noted positive signs in its Medicaid plans. While its leaders told investors that 2026 remains a "trough year" for its Medicaid business, improvements are likely in the cards for 2027.

That trend is supported by state rate setting that more fully accounts for the rising cost of care and the acuity of people enrolled in Medicaid, executives said. The member mix is only expected to increase in acuity as states roll out work requirements at the beginning of this year.

Similarly, Centene CEO Sarah London said the company sees an opportunity for "long-term growth" in the Medicaid market as part of its Q3 report. She noted similar trends around rate setting from states.

London added that the insurer has made significant progress on focus areas for improvement, such as behavioral health.

Centene posted a $71 million loss in the quarter, slimming its losses significantly from $6.6 billion in the prior-year quarter. That massive loss a year ago was backstopped by a one-time impairment charge of more than $6 billion.

Revenues in Q3 were $50.6 billion, up slightly from $49.7 billion in the third quarter of 2025.

Humana offered color on early signals in the Medicare annual enrollment period, which suggests that the insurer is poised for further growth in Medicare Advantage for next year. CEO Jim Rechtin said sign-ups through the early parts of the window indicate 2027 membership growth.

The insurer has had momentum around new sign-ups and has also done well in recapturing enrollments that were impacted by market exits, Rechtin said.

Humana posted a $162 million profit in the third quarter, down from $195 million a year ago. Revenues were $41.15 billion, growing from $32.6 billion in the prior-year quarter.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/digital-health/color-health-launches-clinical-collabs-nci-designated-cancer-centers" hreflang="en">Color Health launches collabs with NCI-designated cancer centers to streamline intake</a>

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Color Health launches collabs with NCI-designated cancer centers to streamline intake" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Color Health, a virtual oncologist-led cancer clinic, has announced formal clinical collaborations with 10 National Cancer Institute-designated cancer centers.

Color and collaborating cancer centers aim to streamline time to treatment through coordinated workup (like imaging and labs) and records preparation, as well as custom intake workflows. These elements help ensure a timely start to care when weeks can be the difference between life or death, executives say. Additional partners will be announced in the coming months.

“Speed to diagnosis, speed to treatment initiation, is one of the single biggest determinants of outcomes in cancer," Caroline Savello, president of Color, told Fierce Healthcare. “Where can we make sure that there is the right clinical coordination, the right navigation coordination happening so that things can happen swiftly through the right processes."

Cancer is among the top drivers of healthcare costs and mortality. Because of its complex and variable nature, the quality and timeliness of care are the best ways to improve outcomes and manage costs. That means less duplicate testing, fewer treatment changes later and fewer avoidable ED visits and hospitalizations

Among the collaborating centers are City of Hope-Los Angeles, Memorial Sloan Kettering, Moffitt Cancer Center, Mount Sinai, Stanford Medicine Cancer Center, UC Davis Comprehensive Cancer Center and UCSF Helen Diller Family Comprehensive Cancer Center. National Cancer Institute (NCI)-designated cancer centers go through a robust evaluation for meeting quality, safety and access standards.

When care at a specific center is determined to be a good fit for a patient, Color coordinates directly to match patients with the right team, completes the clinical workup, reviews medical records and completes authorizations to each center’s specifications. First-appointment targets for most patients are set at seven days or fewer for oncology referrals placed by Color Medical. Additionally, Color verifies insurance coverage to ensure the referral is in-network. Occasionally, Color works with payers or centers on single-case agreements if something is otherwise out of network.

“Color is, above all, a very patient-centered company. When an individual faces a cancer diagnosis for the first time, Color helps to provide the information and navigation to top cancer centers. Their process balances technology with a human touch,” Karen Lu, M.D., physician in chief at Moffitt Cancer Center, told Fierce Healthcare over email.

For any patient issues, Color clinicians escalate to the collaborating centers. Patients also get ongoing wraparound support from Color during and after treatment, including nutrition, mental health, palliative and survivorship care. Additionally, Color will collaborate with the centers to identify any potential clinical trial candidates, gather the needed information and support institution-led eligibility assessment and enrollment.

UCSF Health has already worked with Color for years, so the latest collaboration is an expansion of the partnership.

“[Screening or diagnosis] can be incredibly stressful moments, and navigating a complex healthcare system should not add to that burden,” Laurel Bray-Hanin, VP at UCSF Helen Diller Family Comprehensive Cancer Center, told Fierce Healthcare in emailed comments. “This partnership allows us to connect Color's cancer screening and care coordination efforts directly with UCSF Health's expert patient navigators, who can help patients quickly identify and schedule the appropriate next steps.”

Color “expanded dramatically” this year, per Savello, teaming up with Independence Blue Cross and planning to serve millions more new lives in the new year through employers. Those employers continue to be laser-focused on oncology, per Savello, and are asking Color to set up partnerships with cancer centers in their regions.

“They’re all seeing, obviously, rate increases," she said. "The disease space and the market is very active."

Color partners with local providers to clinically manage all patients through its oncologist-led care team. Its virtual cancer clinic is a national center of excellence certified by the American Society of Clinical Oncology, meaning it meets rigorous standards and is subject to annual audits and reviews. The company, which started working in the cancer space in 2023, says its patients see 66% faster diagnosis and a 77% increase in screening adherence. It also claims to save $62,000 per patient in treatment and closes over three-quarters of costly care gaps.

“Cancer care isn't always a straightforward path. Sometimes, patients need answers about a possible diagnosis, and other times, they need a fresh perspective or access to new treatment options,” Bray-Hanin at UCSF said. “We want patients to feel that someone is looking out for them, helping them understand their options, and connecting them with the expertise and care they need. That commitment to providing compassionate, coordinated care is what makes this partnership so meaningful to us.”

House begins voting on Hegseth impeachment measure

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The House of Representatives began voting Tuesday afternoon on a key procedural measure that could lead to the impeachment of Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth.

The vote comes a week after Democrats trounced Republicans in the first congressional election since the war with Iran started in late February.

Hegseth faces eight articles of impeachment filed by Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., on Monday.

Massie accuses Hegseth of waging war in contravention of Congress, carrying out extrajudicial killings tied to boat strikes in the Caribbean against purported drug smugglers and suppressing free speech by targeting Sen. Mark Kelly, D-Ariz., a military veteran.

Massie is a libertarian who lost his Republican primary in May after clashing with President Donald Trump.

Hegseth, at a press conference at the Pentagon earlier Tuesday, railed against Massie and defended his leadership in the Iran war and other military operations.

"What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it's not accountability. It's a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment," Hegseth said

"It's a farce, and it's a stunt, and everybody knows it," Hegseth said. "It puts a target on the backs of American warriors by signaling that Congress will undermine their missions in real time."This is not patriotism. This is sabotage."

Trump, in a Truth Social post on Tuesday, said Hegseth "gets an 'A' PLUS, PLUS, PLUS for his defense against a disgruntled, angry, defeated, former Congressman, Thomas Massie, a TOTAL LOSER!"

Trump said Monday that he would stand by Hegseth even if the House impeaches him. The president was impeached by the House two times during his first White House term. The Senate acquitted him in both cases.

Republicans hold a slim 218-214 majority in the House.

Some Republicans, notably including Vice President JD Vance, have directly blamed their midterm losses on the Iran war, which continues to see intermittent fighting amid an uneasy stalemate.

If Hegseth is impeached, the Senate would hold a trial and vote on whether to remove him from office. A two-thirds majority is required for conviction and removal.

No Cabinet secretary has ever been removed by the Senate. Just two have been impeached.

The numbers suggest Chelsea are pushovers as they slide down the Premier League table... and club legend GUS POYET says it's time to prove they're up for a fight

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Published: 07:38 EST, 10 November 2026 | Updated: 11:44 EST, 10 November 2026

Morgan Rogers insisted it was no intentional drive-by. That he did not mean to knee Trai Hume in the head while the Sunderland defender was sitting up after a foul. Just an accident, honest.

The history shared between these two particular players opened the incident to interpretation.

Hume had folded Rogers like a deckchair in a 1-1 draw for Aston Villa last season. The challenge went viral. Sunderland shared it on their social media, glamourising it. Rogers responded at the time by saying: 'Great message to send out. Dangerous tackles getting highlight reels.'

At the Stadium of Light on Sunday, Hume was starting to sit up when Rogers caught him. Chelsea were losing 1-0, and frustrated. Their £117million arrival from Villa apologised immediately, so we will believe him when he says it was an innocent collision rather than retribution.

Nevertheless, Rogers does have an aggressive side to him. He squared up with Declan Rice at Arsenal, and James Tarkowski at Everton, and was signed in the summer partially for that passion.

Rogers has contested more duels than any other Chelsea player this season. He loses more than he wins, but is involved, for better or worse. He is Xabi Alonso's most-fouled footballer, while nobody has committed more fouls than this 6ft 2in England international.

Morgan Rogers (right) showed some fight during Chelsea's loss at Sunderland that appears to be lacking across the whole squad

If Rogers' team-mates showed the same mentality they may have avoided defeat on Sunday

Chelsea boss Xabi Alonso needs his side to develop an aggressive streak

So when we ponder whether Alonso's Chelsea are pushovers, Rogers is arguably an exception. He is a hard nut surrounded by a soft shell, and that was a problem at Sunderland.

It was not only that Brian Brobbey bullied Levi Colwill and Wesley Fofana. Regis Le Bris' side were all at it, a team of bouncers ready to rough up the lads planning to party. Cole Palmer and Co ended up getting tossed out of the Stadium of Light on their rears. Chelsea recorded 69 per cent possession - their highest of the Premier League season so far - but were second-best throughout.

Gus Poyet saw that. He played for Chelsea, managed Sunderland, and was an assistant to Dennis Wise at Leeds, who will be the next visitors to Stamford Bridge after this international break.

Daniel Farke's side have won more duels and aerial duels than any other Premier League team this season, which, given what we witnessed on Sunday, should be a concern for Alonso.

Poyet can predict what is coming. Chelsea-Leeds is already a fixture with a narrative attached. It never needs a second invitation to be talked up as 'dirty'. Alonso's side will need to show a willingness to compete if that awaits, more than they managed when travelling to Sunderland.

'Sunderland went to the game with that old-fashioned mentality,' the 58-year-old Uruguayan told Daily Mail Sport. 'Pressure from the fans, noise, tackling, "I'm gonna run over you". Chelsea went on to try to play. There was quite a big difference in that aggression. We see teams that are looking so much for an identity that they forget the process to win games.'

Poyet brought up Chelsea's 2-0 win at Everton last month. We told him how Alonso had admitted it was his favourite performance of the season, describing it as 'solid' and 'serious'.

'There you go,' said Poyet, who thinks before travelling to the Stadium of Light, it would have been worth showing Chelsea's players videos from that victory at the Hill Dickinson. It was a rare showing of seriousness from Alonso's side. Only David Moyes' Everton, with 83, have committed fewer fouls than Chelsea's 95 in the Premier League this season. While giving away free-kicks is hardly a cause for celebration, it is not the worst way of measuring whether you are there to push or be pushed.

Sunderland striker Brian Brobbey (left) bulled Chelsea's Levi Colwill and Wesley Fofana (right)

'For me that was a proper old-fashioned game, being able to win like that,' Poyet said about Chelsea's win at Everton. 'Put the video on and say, "Play the same".

'Now I know what the other side are going to say after watching the Sunderland game. Leeds will be saying, 'Let's go'. Leeds are very solid, and very aggressive, so it can be that kind of game.

'More and more, through the years, I think from the pandemic to now, the difference between the teams is mental. Identity, positions, shapes, blah, blah, blah, but when they are right in here (in the head), it's big. Right now, Leeds, as a team, are a little ahead mentally.'

Alonso is a polite manager. Given the chance to have a pop at the refereeing at the Stadium of Light - how Chelsea were not awarded a penalty when Cole Palmer was sandwiched, but Sunderland were when Brobbey dropped down in front of Colwill - he declined. The Blues boss chose instead to decry his team's defending as substandard. Colwill was hooked at half-time, while there were two new summer signings in the back line in Marco Palestra and Pep Chavarria who need to get to grips with the Premier League, sooner rather than later.

Poyet's first appearance after signing for Chelsea in 1997 was in the Charity Shield. Roy Keane welcomed him to English football with a kick to the head - with a little less subtlety than Rogers' knee on Hume. It finished 1-1 before Manchester United won on penalties.

Ex-Chelsea midfielder Gus Poyet has warned them of the threat Leeds pose later this month

Poyet learned a harsh lesson in that match, and not only from Keane's high kick. 'I couldn't get in the pace of the game,' Poyet recalls. 'I was always late. Ruud (Gullit) at half-time told me, "Gus, you're not really in the front, not really at the back". I said, "I'm trying!" The game was too fast.'

In a weird way, an ACL injury suffered by Poyet helped. He used the recovery time to prepare for returning. 'I went to the stadium, I was watching, learning the language, listening to Dennis Wise in the dressing room, getting that mentality,' he said. 'Then when I came back, I knew everything.

'That was my mentality. Some players need time. Some adjust quickly. You need to use all the time. For example, use this game against Sunderland when you play against Leeds. You cannot say, 'We didn't expect it'. Expect it! It's going to be similar.'

Gritt and MasTec Partner to Bring Physical AI to Solar Construction

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Gritt and MasTec are expanding the use of physical AI on renewable-energy construction sites, starting with utility-scale solar projects across multiple U.S. states. The partnership pairs Gritt’s robotic systems with MasTec Renewables’ construction teams, bringing AI out of planning software and into the movement and installation of materials.

According to the announcement, MasTec evaluated Gritt on active projects where the systems helped install megawatts of solar panels. The companies now plan a pipeline of deployments and will explore applications in other infrastructure sectors. They did not disclose the agreement’s financial terms or identify individual project sites.

For construction robotics, the significance is the operating environment. A solar farm offers thousands of repetitive installation tasks, but those tasks happen amid changing weather, uneven ground, moving equipment, and people working nearby.

Gritt’s approach combines AI with robotic attachments for common construction machinery. In its launch announcement, the company described connecting robotic arms to vehicles such as skid steers and forklifts to perform material handling and assembly. This adds automation to familiar equipment rather than requiring contractors to introduce an entirely new vehicle platform.

The company emerged publicly in July with $32.4 million in pre-seed and Series A financing, including a $26 million Series A led by Obvious Ventures. The MasTec agreement follows that launch and an earlier solar-construction partnership with Burns & McDonnell.

Gritt’s solar product page identifies panel pick-and-place as a current application, with purlin assembly, component transport, and oversight among its expansion areas. It reports more than 35,000 panels installed and more than 18 MW of solar installed. Its separate 2.8 GW contracted-construction figure describes future work under contract, not completed installations.

In a technical explanation of its architecture, Gritt describes a foundation model trained on labeled and unlabeled images and video from construction environments. That shared representation supports task-specific models, including control policies, segmentation, and six-degree-of-freedom pose estimation—identifying an object’s position and orientation in three-dimensional space.

The company combines learning with checks on execution. Observation systems assess uncertainty and validate proposed actions, while new models first operate in shadow mode before taking control. Takeover and recovery mechanisms allow work to continue when the system needs help and generate examples for subsequent training.

Gritt also describes a real-to-simulation-to-real pipeline: site images and video help reconstruct scenes, which become simulated training and validation episodes. CAD models, reconstructed objects, and randomized conditions let the company revisit unusual situations without depending entirely on encountering them again on a live site.

These mechanisms address a central deployment challenge: a robot must handle unfamiliar conditions without turning every unexpected event into a damaged component or a stopped project. The engineering goal is repeatable performance under variation, not simply a successful demonstration.

MasTec Renewables’ solar business spans development and design through construction and operations. Its capabilities include photovoltaic system modeling, materials logistics, foundation work, electrical collection systems, substations, and high-voltage connections. It installs fixed-mount systems as well as single- and dual-axis tracking configurations.

That breadth puts robotic panel installation within a much larger delivery process. A machine’s placement speed matters only if crews, materials, foundations, and electrical work remain coordinated. An attachment that performs well in isolation still has to fit the contractor’s sequencing, maintenance, and safety practices.

MasTec Renewables Vice President of Construction Rob Turner said the company’s evaluation showed an opportunity to combine its workforce’s experience with Gritt’s technology. The companies frame the rollout around supporting skilled crews, with people continuing to guide and validate work.

Gritt’s published research also illustrates why outdoor robotics safety requires attention to the training pipeline. In an analysis of worker detection, the company examined how automated data-quality filters can disproportionately discard images of kneeling, crouching, or partly obscured people.

Those positions are ordinary on construction sites, even if they appear infrequently in a filtered dataset. A bounding box may correctly identify a worker while a pose estimator struggles to reconstruct the person’s joints. Rejecting the whole example can remove exactly the cases a deployed robot needs to recognize.

Gritt reported that adding simulated workers in mined working postures improved detection recall for low poses by 1.6 percentage points on real site footage. The result concerns that specific perception evaluation; it is not a measure of the MasTec deployment’s overall safety performance.

Gritt’s broader ambition is to connect physical execution with site verification and planning. Its systems collect information while working, creating the possibility of comparing what has actually been built with what a project expects to complete next.

For MasTec, the immediate application remains solar construction. For Gritt, repeated deployments offer a route to adapting its technology across more tasks and conditions. The partnership’s next test is operational: whether useful automation can be introduced consistently across projects while preserving the judgment and coordination that construction crews provide.

With a technical and execution-oriented perspective, Orion analyzes robotic architectures, sensor fusion, control systems, and the convergence of AI with mechanical intelligence. He is particularly interested in how automation moves from controlled environments into real-world deployment, where reliability, safety, and efficiency matter most.

Your AI Agents Need Job Descriptions, Not Better Prompts

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ask a company what a new accounts payable analyst is allowed to do and you’ll get a precise answer. She works in these systems, with these vendors, and anything over an approval limit of, say, $10,000 goes to her manager for a signature. She’s on probation for her first few months. All of that usually sits in a delegation-of-authority table that internal audit checks every year.

Ask the same company what its new invoice agent is allowed to do and you’ll often get a screenshot of a prompt.

That gap bothers me more than any model benchmark. We’re hiring a digital workforce without the management system we spent a century building for people: nobody writes down its authority, nobody tests its judgment before it starts, and nobody has practiced switching it off.

A prompt describes intent. It says what the agent is for. It rarely says what the agent may never do, where its limits sit, or when it has to stop and hand the decision to a person.

And when a prompt does say those things, nothing enforces them. A line telling an agent not to issue refunds over $500 is a request to a probabilistic system. No auditor would sign off on “we asked the analyst nicely” as a control for a human, so I don’t see why we accept it for software.

Security people already have a name for the result. The OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications lists Excessive Agency as a core risk, rooted in excessive functionality, excessive permissions, or excessive autonomy. In plain terms, it’s an employee handed the keys to everything on day one. The OWASP guidance reads like a good manager’s checklist: give the agent only the tools it needs, scope its permissions, require human approval for high-impact actions, and enforce authorization in the downstream systems instead of trusting the model to police itself.

I’d underline that last one. The grant has to live where the action happens, in the agent’s own service identity, its tool permissions, and the spend cap at the API gateway. Running agents on a shared service account is like five people sharing one badge. When something goes wrong, the audit log says the service account did it, and you’re left guessing which agent.

Gartner predicts over 40% of agentic AI projects will be canceled by the end of 2027, citing escalating costs, unclear business value, and inadequate risk controls.

Hype and immature products are part of that story. Still, try reading the three causes the way a COO would. Costs escalate when nobody set a budget. Value stays unclear when nobody wrote down what the work was supposed to produce. Risk controls fall short when there are no limits, no reviews, and nobody clearly accountable. Gartner doesn’t frame it this way; that’s my reading. It’s the same thing that happens when people are set loose without a management system, and that problem is a lot older than AI.

The frameworks see the same hole. The NIST AI Risk Management Framework treats Govern as the function that runs through Map, Measure, and Manage, and its Generative AI Profile carries that into generative systems. What frameworks can’t give you is the working machinery, the grants and tests and reviews that a team actually runs on a Tuesday. Without those, governance stays a PDF on a shared drive.

Start with the job description, which for an agent becomes an authority grant. Before an agent touches a live system, someone should be able to write one page covering what it may do, what it may never do, its approval thresholds, which human owns it, and who can widen its limits. In the RACI, the accountable party is a person. If you can’t write that page, the role isn’t ready to fill, and you’d say the same about a human hire.

Two old rules from delegation of authority carry straight over. You can’t delegate authority you don’t hold, so a sub-agent should never get a wider grant than the agent that spawned it. Segregation of duties still applies too. An agent that can both create a vendor and pay that vendor is the same audit finding it would be for a person.

Then there’s the hiring test. We mostly test agents the way we’d test a calculator, by checking whether the answer is right. A test for a worker would check whether it stayed inside its grant, asked for approval when it was supposed to, escalated when the situation went beyond its brief, and stopped when it should have.

In my own research I’ve been building a benchmark of 50 scenarios around exactly these failures: crossing approval limits, acting on stale data, following injected instructions that widen scope, keeping access after a role change. So far I’ve only run it against scripted agents, to check that the scenarios behave as designed, so I have no model results to report. What surprised me while writing them was how ordinary they are. They’re the normal ways employees go off-brief, rewritten for software. Most public leaderboards still score task completion, and very few score whether the agent respected its authority.

Probation and promotion translate fairly directly. With people, we start them narrow and widen their scope as they prove their judgment. Agents can earn their limits the same way, through a record of staying in bounds, escalating well, and stopping cleanly, and that record can just as easily justify narrowing a grant.

Agents add a wrinkle people don’t. When a vendor upgrades the underlying model, you’ve effectively got a new hire wearing the old badge. I’d treat that as a change event and re-test before the grant carries over.

Termination has to be rehearsed before you need it. If you had to stop this agent and pull its access in the next hour, could you? Who has the authority to make that call at 2 a.m.? What breaks downstream, and which in-flight commitments does someone have to unwind by hand? If nobody has run that drill, you’re hoping the kill switch works.

You can hold an employee responsible. You can’t hold an agent responsible for anything, and that’s where the comparison stops working. It’s also why the grant has to be enforced in systems, and why every agent needs a named person who owns it. Someone ends up accountable either way. Usually it’s whoever signed off, whether they knew they were signing or not.

Some of these agents will eventually do something they shouldn’t. When that happens, the post-incident review will ask what it already asks about people. What was it allowed to do, and where is that written? Who approved widening its access? How long did it take to stop? Who owned it?

If the honest answers are “a prompt,” “nobody remembers,” “hours,” and “unclear,” a better model wouldn’t have saved you.

Models are becoming rented inputs, swapped and upgraded on a vendor’s schedule. Two companies can run the same model and end up in very different places. One has agents with budgets, limits, owners, and an audit trail for every consequential action, while the other has an expensive pilot headed for someone’s cancellation statistics.

The difference is the management system around the model. That gets built slowly, mostly by people who have run organizations before.

If your newest agent went past its authority tomorrow, would anything in your company notice? And could you show an auditor, in writing, where the boundary was?

Nitin Gupta is Global Product Owner, Commercial Analytics at Mondelēz International. He has spent more than 15 years building data and AI products, and he writes about how companies decide what AI is allowed to do and who answers for it. He is a LinkedIn Top Voice.

I found a hidden Android tool that solves all my tiny text troubles

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > Android

When the print on your Android display is too small to read, and the pinch-to-zoom feature isn't working as expected, try this.

How many times have you been on your phone, wanted to read something (a piece of news, a website, etc.), only to find the text is too small and every effort to zoom in doesn’t work?

Also: 7 Android accessibility features that can make your life easier

This happens quite often when I’m viewing content in the Google News app — especially in the morning, when my eyes are still in “wake up” mode. It can be frustrating. And it’s not only text. Sometimes there’s an image I need to enlarge, but for whatever reason, pinch-to-zoom isn’t working on that particular screen.

Fortunately, there’s an option you can enable to ensure you can always zoom in on your Android device, no matter what’s on the screen. The option is found in Accessibility and is aptly called Magnification. With this feature enabled, you can quickly zoom in on the screen to enlarge any displayed content. It doesn’t matter what’s on the screen or what app is showing the image, page, or text — Magnification will magnify it.

This iteration of the feature has been available since Android 15. I found it by accident while poking around the Settings page. Since then, I’ve used it quite a bit, and it’s eased my morning frustration with reading news sites.

The Magnification feature is quite simple: once enabled, a magnifier shortcut appears on the screen. Tap that button, and whatever is on your screen will increase in size. It’s really that easy. One caveat: As long as the Magnification shortcut is enabled, the button remains on your screen. You can move the button up and down or to the left or right side of the display, but if you want the feature enabled, the button must remain.

The good news, however, is that the button does fade a bit, so it’s not glaring and in your face. You can still see it, but it gets just enough transparency that it doesn’t stand out all that much.

Also: Android is getting my favorite iOS tracking feature

Open Settings by dragging the Notification Shade down twice, then tapping the gear icon (or tapping Settings in the App Drawer). Where this is located depends on the Android version you’re using and your device. For example, on my Pixel 9 Pro, it’s in Settings > Accessibility > Magnification. On a RedMagic 12 Pro, it’s in Settings > Features > Accessibility. If you don’t find it right away, open Settings and search for Magnification.

Once you’ve found the Magnification option, tap the On/Off slider until it’s in the On position, and the Magnifier button will appear on your screen. Tap the button, and whatever is on the display will enlarge enough that you shouldn’t have any trouble reading it.

Here’s a little tip: After tapping the Magnification button, whatever’s been enlarged can be scrolled vertically, but not horizontally. Fortunately, there’s a way to ensure that you can read everything on the page.

Also: I use Google Magnifier to read tiny print without my glasses

Tap the gear icon at the bottom right of the display to open the options pop-up. On this page, tap the “Magnifier size” icon that’s second from the right. This enables a movable magnifier you can place where you need it (kind of like a traditional magnifying glass). You can also change the size of the movable magnifier (by tapping one of the other “Magnifier size” buttons) as well as the size of the text when magnified (by dragging the bottom slider).

Also: RedMagic 12 Pro is the fastest Android phone I’ve ever seen

This feature has alleviated the early-morning frustration of having to squint (even with my glasses on) to read certain news sites. Give it a try and see if it doesn’t make your Android life a bit clearer.

OpenAI Study Finds AI Freelance Projects Carry Larger Budgets

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

OpenAI on November 10, 2026 published joint research with Upwork showing that AI-related fixed-price freelance postings carried budgets 52.4% higher than comparable non-AI postings, while the adjusted gap in advertised hourly rates was only 1.5%.

The full report, The Rebundling of Freelance Work in the AI Age, treats that contrast as evidence that project scope and the quantity of labor purchased matter more than a simple hourly premium. All three adjusted estimates compare work within the same subcategory and posting month. Among hired hourly contracts with positive recorded hours, AI-related work involved 40.6% more hours during the first 90 days after hire, with observations through April 2026.

The study classifies a posting as AI-related when it requests AI skills, requires AI tools or involves improving AI-generated output. A fixed-price budget covers an entire deliverable, while the hourly measure is the midpoint of the client’s advertised range, separating the price of time from the amount of work purchased.

Content writing provides the clearest contrast between direct AI use and freelance hiring. The study reports that it accounted for 14.5% of sampled personal and work-related ChatGPT conversations from January 2025 through June 2026, compared with 1.3% of Upwork postings in the first half of 2026. Its share of Upwork postings fell roughly 31% between the first halves of 2025 and 2026.

The report’s wider comparison supplies contrasting cases: video and animation represented 1.0% of sampled ChatGPT conversations but 10.0% of Upwork postings, while mobile development accounted for 0.2% and 7.8%, respectively. Relative ChatGPT intensity compares category shares in ChatGPT during the second half of 2025 with an Upwork baseline in the first half of 2023.

Across 63 matched subcategories, 43 either had lower relative ChatGPT intensity and gained marketplace share or had higher intensity and lost share. Six categories combined higher intensity with share gains and 14 combined lower intensity with losses, leaving 20 outside the simpler substitution pattern. Direct AI use alone therefore does not explain the changing composition of freelance demand.

The report’s task analysis shows the effective number of clusters in AI-related postings rising from 14.6 in January–June 2023 to 21.2 in July–December 2025, approximately 45%. The measure expresses diversity as the equivalent number of equally represented clusters, capturing how work spread beyond a few dominant activities.

Within individual postings, the study found an average of 3.35 distinct task clusters in AI-related jobs, compared with 3.19 in other jobs. AI-related postings were more likely to contain three or four tasks, with no corresponding increase in the five-or-more category. Among jobs with at least two tasks, historically uncommon pairs accounted for an average 8.4% of pairs in AI-related postings and 2.4% in non-AI postings; uncommon means the pair appeared together in fewer than 200 pre-AI postings.

Examples in the report combine AI-generated video with editing and motion graphics, or chatbot development with application integration, to deliver a finished outcome. Social-media campaign management, AI-generated video, marketing automation and AI API integration gained share within AI-related work from 2023 to 2025. Building chatbots and machine-learning models remained the largest cluster, although its share of task assignments declined from 21.8% to 17.8%.

The ChatGPT comparison used 36,896 conversations sampled from consumer accounts and classified as personal or work-related. It excluded schoolwork, unclear-purpose conversations and conversations without an Upwork taxonomy match. GPT 5.6 Luna classified each conversation using up to its first five available user messages; the report states that no researcher read user messages or examined individual user data.

Upwork grouped more than 3,000 activity descriptions into more than 400 standardized task clusters. A GPT-powered pipeline evaluated each posting’s title, description, requested skills, taxonomy and other context to classify AI-related work. The category analysis tracks share changes through the second half of 2025 and does not measure aggregate marketplace growth. More broadly, the two datasets are not directly matched, neither represents the labor market as a whole, and the estimates describe associations rather than causal effects.

For separate market context, Upwork’s July 14, 2026 Future Workforce Index drew partly on a March–April 2026 survey of 2,400 U.S.-based skilled workers. It reported that 38% of skilled U.S. knowledge workers freelance, up from 28% the prior year. That survey is distinct from the joint report and does not supply its platform estimates.

The authors’ broader interpretation is that freelance value may increasingly lie in connecting tools, applying domain judgment and delivering finished outcomes. On these platforms, project scope and integration therefore carry as much of the AI labor-market signal as demand for any single activity.

With an analytical and human-centered perspective, Jade examines how data-driven tools are applied to hiring processes, skills mapping, performance analytics, and workforce planning—while paying close attention to fairness, bias mitigation, and employee well-being. She is particularly interested in how organizations balance efficiency gains with ethical responsibility and trust when deploying AI in people-centric systems.

Canaan (CAN) Swings to an $8 Million Profit on Bitcoin Gains as Rig Sales Collapse 96%

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Canaan Inc. (NASDAQ: CAN) swung to $8.0 million of third-quarter net income on a $51.9 million fair-value gain in its bitcoin holdings and derivatives, the mining-rig maker said Tuesday, even as machine sales collapsed 96%.

The company filed the unaudited results Tuesday in a 6-K with the Securities and Exchange Commission. Total revenue fell to $14.5 million from $150.5 million a year earlier, including $4.8 million from product sales and $9.5 million from mining. Canaan sold 1.0 million terahashes per second of computing power and mined 133 bitcoin.

The profit did not come from the business. Canaan posted an $8.6 million gross loss and a $42.9 million operating loss. Chief financial officer Jin “James” Cheng said a recovery in bitcoin prices toward quarter-end generated the $51.9 million fair-value gain and called it “distinct from underlying operating performance.”

The $51.9 million is the sum of a $20.1 million gain on cryptocurrency and a $31.9 million gain on financial derivatives. Canaan also reported $22.4 million of non-GAAP adjusted EBITDA, a figure that still includes those gains. Strip them out and adjusted EBITDA would have been a loss of about $29.5 million.

Nangeng Zhang, Canaan’s chairman and chief executive officer, pointed to “continued weakness in bitcoin mining equipment demand and intense industry pricing competition.” Products revenue dropped from $13.6 million in the second quarter and from $118.6 million in the same period of 2025. The company blamed lower volumes and a lower average selling price.

Canaan took $8.2 million of inventory-related write-downs, down from $25.3 million in the second quarter. Mining operations posted a gross margin of about 30% before depreciation, with an all-in power cost of about $0.043 per kilowatt-hour and roughly 10 exahashes per second of installed capacity outside joint ventures.

The company held 1,875.5 bitcoin as of Sept. 30. Of that, 517.5 coins were owned outright, 1,258 were pledged for secured term loans and 100 sat in a fixed-term product. It booked $43.7 million of cryptocurrency assets and $114.2 million of cryptocurrency receivables.

The stack is down about 40 bitcoin from the 1,915.5 Canaan reported at the end of the second quarter, even after the company mined 133 in the period, implying it moved roughly 173 bitcoin off the treasury. Zhang said Canaan is “selectively monetizing cryptocurrency holdings to fund share repurchases.”

On Nov. 6, the board approved a new $100 million share repurchase authorization over 12 months and launched an initial $20 million program under that cap.

Canaan is trying to recast itself around power and compute infrastructure. Its 1-gigawatt project in Duval County, Texas, received a conditional “Studied Load – Firm” classification under ERCOT’s Batch Zero process, a grid-connection step Zhang said remains “subject to further studies and approvals.” A second 1-gigawatt project in Zapata County is in early-stage development.

To support those plans, Canaan set aside $50 million of restricted cash as collateral for a letter of credit tied to infrastructure contracts and made about $3.3 million of project prepayments. In October it bought a parcel of U.S. land for about $1.1 million. Cash ended Sept. 30 at $72.8 million, $50 million of it restricted, leaving $22.8 million unrestricted.

Canaan also plans a 1-for-25 reverse ADS split, effective on or about Dec. 4, changing each ADS from 15 Class A ordinary shares to 375. The move is meant to help the company regain compliance with Nasdaq’s minimum bid price requirement.

For the fourth quarter, Canaan expects revenue of $18 million to $22 million.

The shift toward power and compute puts Canaan in the same lane as other public bitcoin miners. MARA sold 13,597 bitcoin to fund AI infrastructure, and Hyperscale Data sold its last coins for a Michigan data center.

Whether a paper gain on 1,875 bitcoin and a pair of early-stage Texas power projects can carry Canaan until customers start buying machines again is the question the quarter leaves open.
