Graphon Labs Raises $35M Series A to Scale Persistent AI Memory

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Graphon Labs announced a $35 million Series A on November 10, 2026 to scale persistent-memory infrastructure for AI agents across enterprise, developer and on-device products. RRE Ventures led the round, with HP, Energy Impact Partners, Hitachi Ventures and Gaia VC participating; Graphon’s Series A release put total funding at $43.3 million.

Chief executive Arbaaz Khan said the capital will move the company from early experimentation to scaled enterprise deployments, open its infrastructure to developers, accelerate research and support on-device memory.

RRE general partner Raju Rishi said the firm sees Graphon becoming a source of truth beneath agents, explaining: “Models can reason, and agents can act, but neither gets better if they cannot build off of prior knowledge.”

Graphon calls its approach Persistent Intelligence, meaning AI that can continuously access, understand, remember and build on earlier information. The company says retrieval-augmented generation, GraphRAG, context windows and conventional retrieval can compress or fragment relationships, history and temporal understanding as information grows and changes. Its platform instead ingests video, images, audio and documents, automatically building an ontology—a structured representation of the data and its connections—while retaining links to sources.

Khan’s technical explanation divides the architecture into two pieces: a detailed graph representing information and relationships, and a compressed graphon representation that guides retrieval through that larger structure. Graphons are a mathematical framework for very large graphs, and the founders developed the underlying research during their PhDs at Penn’s GRASP Lab. The engineering goals include incorporating new information, tracing evidence back to its origin and operating at practical cost and speed.

Developers use S3-style storage and search concepts such as buckets, objects, files and queries without needing to understand the mathematics. The interface supports keyword, semantic and image searches, plus agent-assisted questions across multiple sources. A simple lookup and a deeper investigation therefore draw on the same platform, reducing the need to assemble a separate retrieval stack for each kind of question.

Graphon reports 81.7% accuracy on ATM-Bench, a University of Cambridge benchmark for question answering across video, audio, images and text. The reported result is 5.0 percentage points ahead of the next system on the public leaderboard.

The architecture supports three products. Graphon Enterprise provides governed memory across enterprise data and can be hosted by Graphon, deployed in a Graphon-managed or customer-managed virtual private cloud, or installed on-premises. Graphon Developer is intended for builders working across data types without recreating the context stack, while Graphon On-Device Memory targets phones, laptops, cameras, wearables and other devices.

Adoption differs among the customers named in the announcement. Although the release broadly describes deployments with the San Francisco 49ers, Hitachi and other large enterprises, 49ers technology executive Costa Kladianos said the organization is exploring applications such as uncovering sponsorship opportunities and supporting internal teams. Hitachi Rail’s Amaury Jourdan described an evaluation showing promise for contextual-knowledge discovery, automated data-source connections and improved AI inference in its industrial setting.

Graphon’s company profile lists July 2025 as its founding date and Khan, chief technology officer Clark Zhang and chief operating officer Deepak Mishra as its founders. The San Francisco company emerged from stealth on May 14, 2026 with an $8.3 million seed round led by Arvind Gupta of Novera Ventures.

The seed announcement identified South Korean conglomerate GS as an early enterprise customer. Its representative cited applications including analysis of customer movement in convenience stores and CCTV-based construction-safety work.

The Series A therefore funds a commercialization test as well as a research program. Graphon must turn exploratory enterprise work into scaled deployments, persuade outside developers to build on its memory layer and adapt the same architecture for private on-device use.

AMD and Cornelis Expand HPC Partnership as GWDG Deploys EMMY P4

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AMD and Cornelis are expanding their high-performance computing partnership, pairing AMD processors and accelerators with Cornelis networking to help research organizations bring larger scientific workloads into production. A deployment at Germany’s GWDG provides a concrete example: the EMMY P4 supercomputer combines approximately 300 dual-socket AMD EPYC 9745 nodes with Cornelis CN5000 Omni-Path networking.

The collaboration extends beyond selling compatible components. The companies are establishing a joint go-to-market program covering system design, application benchmarking, validation, acceptance testing, and production deployment through existing manufacturers and system integrators. Customers can also test their own applications at AMD’s HPC Center of Excellence in Munich before committing to a configuration.

That approach addresses a persistent supercomputing problem: buying more processors does not guarantee proportionally faster research. Applications have to move data between those processors quickly enough to keep them working.

Scientific simulations often divide a large problem across many servers. Each server calculates part of the result, then exchanges information with the others. Computational fluid dynamics, for example, requires neighboring regions of a simulated flow to share updated conditions as the calculation progresses.

Those exchanges make network latency, message-handling capacity, and congestion consequential. A processor waiting for another node’s data contributes little useful computation, however fast it performs arithmetic. Message Passing Interface, or MPI, is a widely used software interface for coordinating this distributed work.

Cornelis supplies the interconnect fabric connecting the nodes, while AMD supplies the compute. The expanded program aims to validate those pieces together against applications rather than treating network bandwidth and processor specifications as sufficient evidence of system performance.

Lisa Spelman, CEO of Cornelis, said each new EPYC generation increases the number of cores needing access to the network. Her point is that adding compute capacity can leave expensive resources waiting if communication cannot keep pace, making workload-specific validation essential.

GWDG, the scientific computing organization in Göttingen, supports NHR@Göttingen, one of Germany’s nine national high-performance computing centers. Its users run applications spanning life sciences, physics, quantum research, AI, and the humanities. Cornelis says the broader environment handles approximately 3.5 million HPC jobs annually.

AMD’s EPYC 9745 specifications list 128 physical cores per processor, putting 256 cores in each dual-socket node. Keeping that many cores productive across hundreds of nodes makes communication efficiency a system-level concern.

GWDG’s installation documentation describes an initial group of 252 nodes with 768 GiB of DDR5 memory and 3.8 TiB of local NVMe storage per node, connected through CN5000 adapters with 200G links. The announcement describes the larger EMMY P4 deployment as approximately 300 nodes.

The distinction between this CPU installation and the partnership’s GPU work matters. EMMY P4 demonstrates EPYC and Cornelis networking in a scientific computing environment; it does not mean the announced CPU nodes contain AMD Instinct accelerators.

Cornelis says CN5000 is qualified for production with AMD EPYC 9005 processors across more than 25 validated platform configurations in North America and Europe. The companies have also extended CN5000 qualification to AMD Instinct MI350X Series accelerators.

For buyers, application testing can reveal whether an architecture fits the work that actually runs in their organization. A large simulation with frequent synchronization can behave differently from an AI workload that spends more time inside accelerator kernels. Memory capacity, software libraries, node configuration, and network behavior all influence the result.

Cornelis reports that a selected set of 132 CONVERGE computational fluid dynamics workloads achieved 20% higher average throughput on four nodes than a comparison using NVIDIA NDR InfiniBand. It also reports nearly ninefold scaling from one to eight nodes for a STAR-CCM+ workload running on EPYC 9755 processors.

These are vendor-reported application results from particular test configurations. The supporting Cornelis technical white papers are the relevant reference for test conditions; the figures do not establish the same advantage for every code or cluster. The practical value of the Munich testing program is that customers can assess their own workloads against that backdrop.

The companies are also preparing for another hardware generation. Cornelis positions its upcoming CN6000 around an 800G adapter and a PCIe 6.0 x16 interface, with OEM systems combining CN6000 and AMD EPYC 9006 processors expected in the first quarter of 2027. Those systems are a roadmap item, separate from the deployed CN5000-based EMMY P4 infrastructure.

AMD’s accelerator strategy also includes the ROCm software stack, which provides tools and libraries for GPU computing. Supporting scientific AI requires that software layer as well as the processors and network: researchers need applications, mathematical libraries, and communication software that work together.

Traditional scientific computing also places substantial emphasis on double-precision, or FP64, arithmetic. That differs from the lower-precision calculations used in many AI workloads, so a platform’s suitability depends on more than its headline AI performance.

The expanded AMD–Cornelis partnership is ultimately a bid to make complete research systems easier to evaluate and deploy. GWDG supplies a production example of the CPU-and-network combination, while joint application testing and future accelerator support broaden the potential use cases. For research centers, the consequential measure is how quickly a validated system completes useful work—not how many processors it contains.

3 AI agents value a home similarly, could they be wrong?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Three AI valuation systems can produce similar home values while relying on the same market definition and comparable selection assumptions. Agreement does not validate accuracy if the competitive market area is misidentified.  AI Summary

The similarities in the estimations made by three independent systems might be perceived as confirmation of accuracy. However, when AI-powered systems operate under the same assumptions regarding a property’s competitive market, the agreement might bring no additional assurance.

Imagine three different AI agents estimating the value of your house. The first system offers a $1.59 million valuation. The second – $1.61 million. And the third – $1.60 million.

It looks like three independent systems agree on the price. This information can be quite reassuring for the homeowner who plans to sell. Moreover, the similarity in the numbers can be influential for the asking price.

However, this question comes to mind: Have three different systems produced the same number because they analyzed the data independently, or is the result just similar due to the same initial assumptions?

Let’s talk about this case in more details and figure out whether agreement can increase the probability of a mistaken valuation.

The issue can arise long before any of the numbers is calculated by the systems.

The real estate sector accumulates huge volumes of property data. Closed transactions, MLS records, active listings, public records and other sources provide rich information about the prices, property features and market activity.

Modern valuation models use highly advanced statistical techniques and machine learning algorithms to make sense of this information. AI agents can extract and analyze property records, use third-party valuations or generate an accurate price explanation based on the market data.

However, the three independent systems can be based on the same underlying sources, analytical methods or similar understanding of the competitive market. And, in case the initial assumptions are incorrect, the similarity of results cannot provide any additional assurance.

Let’s imagine a condominium in Miami on the 24th floor of a high-rise building.

AI generates valuation based on the recent transactions of condominiums with the same square footage, number of bedrooms and geographical proximity. It uses the information to make calculations and produce a detailed valuation.

Everything looks good. Properties exist. Transaction histories are true. Property characteristics match the search criteria. The calculations are accurate. But do these properties compete in the same market?

In dense residential markets like Miami, geographical proximity and similar property characteristics can hide significant differences in competitive positioning.

Two condominiums may have the same number of bedrooms, the same square footage, and even be located right across the street from each other. But one may be located in the entry-level residential community while another – in the waterfront development.

They may have identical property types, but different price ranges, ownership costs, amenities, views, and other characteristics that are relevant for the market analysis.

On paper, the properties look comparable. But in reality, they may attract different buyers and be in competition with different properties.

This concept is well understood by established organizations like Fannie Mae. According to the Appraiser Update of December 2024, two properties that are located side by side can be in the same neighborhood, but occupy different market areas since they appeal to different buyers.

According to the guidelines on the comparable sales, the comparable properties should compete for the same buyers as the subject property.

This is an important difference between searching similar property records and identifying relevant market evidence.

Competitive market area may begin with the condominium building where the subject property is located, include similar units within the same development, or some competing residential communities in the area. Price range classification and property characteristics can help with the organization of this analysis, but they cannot substitute the process itself.

The market area should be defined with the help of buyer alternatives, not the geographical proximity.

I have worked with residential property data and analyzed the range of automated valuation models and AI-generated valuations, and the distinction between preparing property records and organizing the market analysis keeps coming back.

Property records can contain accurate addresses, standardized property types, verified square footage, complete transaction history, and precise geographic coordinates. All of the duplicate records are deleted, inconsistencies are corrected, and missing information is added.

These are important steps in data preparation. But clean data does not tell anything about the competition between the residential communities, the price range segments they occupy, and the relevant properties for the market analysis.

This requires one more step: organizing residential communities, analyzing their characteristics, establishing the relations between the properties, and validating those relations on the market evidence.

These processes can be automated with the help of machine learning. But the underlying definitions and inferences require testing and maintenance as well. It does not mean that current valuation models are based on radius searches. Many of them use the much more complicated methods and can find the complex relationships between property characteristics, locations, and market activity.

The challenge here is more fundamental: can we make sure that the information used to support the valuation is the appropriate representation of the competitive market area of the subject property?

Larger dataset, more advanced model, or a better explanation of the results cannot guarantee this. More property data does not mean more market evidence automatically.

Defining market areas has its consequences not only for comparable sales, but for the statistics describing the supply, demand, and the current competitive conditions as well.

Inventory, speed of selling, days on market and months of supply depend on which properties are included in the calculation. Let’s take a simplified, hypothetical example.

Imagine a competitive market area that consists of a group of competing condominium communities with 12 active listings. This market area has been experiencing three sales per month. So, the inventory represents four months of supply.

Now, let’s imagine an AI system combining these properties with another residential area with 48 active listings and three sales per month.

Combined market area will include 60 active listings and six monthly sales, resulting in 10 months of supply.

Both calculations are mathematically correct. But the second number describes a different set of properties, not necessarily the competitive environment of the first market area.

For the homeowner, the difference is important. Four months of supply and 10 months of supply can indicate very different competitive conditions. Depending on the way of interpretation, this statistic may have impact on the pricing expectations and the decision whether to change the asking price.

Of course, the actual market analysis requires consistency of the measurement periods, sufficient transaction activity, and the consideration of changing conditions. Small samples can lead to uncertainties, even in case of correct market definition.

But the example shows an important point. Calculation can be mathematically correct, but the population of properties that is included in the calculation can be wrong for the particular decision.

The same concerns median prices, selling speed, and other market indicators. If the dataset includes communities from different competitive segments, its summary statistics may cover the completely irrelevant competitive conditions.

That is why market definition and maintenance of relevant residential communities is not just an improvement of comparable selection. This is an essential part of market analysis.

Research in other industries provides one more argument to be skeptical about the opinion agreement of several AI systems.

The 2025 study Correlated Errors in Large Language Models published at the International Conference on Machine Learning has examined more than 350 language models and has found out substantial correlations in their mistakes.

On one benchmark, when both models produced a mistake, they chose the same wrong answer in 60% of cases. Correlations were also found between models of different architecture and provided by different organizations.

However, the study was not concerned about the home valuations, and its results do not prove that AI pricing agents routinely make the same mistakes. But it reveals an important limitation of apparent model independence: different models can make correlated mistakes.

In case of property valuations, the risk of correlated mistakes is associated with shared assumptions.

If several AI systems analyze overlapping market information, use similar property-selection criteria, or rely on the same initial valuation source, their conclusions may be less independent than they seem.

And when the underlying market is improperly defined, these systems can reinforce misleading interpretation without generating a single false record.

It is important to remember that this situation differs from the usual problem of AI hallucinations. Hallucination provides some unsupported or invented information. Market definition mistake can produce misleading conclusion on the basis of the correct data.

Verifying the existence of transactions is necessary. But it does not guarantee that these transactions are really relevant for the analysis.

The most valuable AI pricing advice should do more than producing a number. It should help homeowners to understand the market evidence behind the number, assumptions and uncertainties associated with it.

First of all, it is the market itself. Which residential communities and competing properties have been considered as relevant, and why? Does the analysis distinguish between the properties that are geographically close and actually compete with the same buyers?

Then it is the evidence itself. Which closed transactions serve as the base for the recommended price? How the characteristics of the subject property compare to these sales? Which active listings show the current competition? Were the relevant transactions excluded, or less comparable properties were included just due to the geographical proximity?

Understanding of the limitations of the system is also important.

Model can have limited transaction activity in the subject’s competitive market. The particular unit may have some unique characteristics. Comparable properties may require adjustments, or the market conditions have already changed after the closing of the most relevant transactions.

In these circumstances, admitting the uncertainty is more valuable than producing an artificial precise conclusion.

Also, homeowners should be able to distinguish between explanation and validation. System can provide a good explanation of its estimation, but this fact does not confirm the predictive accuracy of the method.

Where possible, evidence of how a valuation method performs against actual sales prices for comparable properties and market segments can be more valuable than the agreement of several AI-generated estimates.

Not every automated valuation model operates with a conventional list of comparable sales, and not every proprietary system allows checking its inner workings. This is the reason why transparency of available evidence, limitations, and performance metrics is especially important.

For the homeowner receiving three similar estimates, the next step should be to check whether they are based on the different market evidence or they just reflect the same initial data.

Agreement may be reassuring. Understanding of the reasons for it is more valuable.

As AI becomes more and more accessible, the ability to retrieve property records, calculate statistics, and generate the polished price explanations will become commonplace.

The meaningful differentiation will consist in the quality of the market intelligence. It includes reliable property data, well-organized residential communities, relevant relations between the competing properties, consistent market definitions, and methods that can be validated.

Neither proprietary dataset nor publicly available records are superior. What matters is how this information is organized, validated, maintained, and applied to solve the particular problem.

Technological sophistication of models will continue to grow, but it should not be mistaken for independent verification.

For the homeowners preparing to sell, the consequences are practical. Pricing decision can impact the way of entering the market, the competition of the property with alternatives, its listing period, and the negotiating position of the seller.

The purpose is not replacing the professional judgment with the impressive number. It is providing homeowners and real estate professionals with the better evidence and the more precise market context.

Three AI agents valuing your home similarly sound convincing. However, before trusting their conclusion, one more question is important to ask.

Is the market they are analyzing the right one?

Jake Miakota is the CEO of Subdivisions.com, a residential community-based real estate market intelligence platform.

Why Cardano’s 57% staked supply signals resilience despite ADA price dip

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Coinbase data showed that 57% of the Cardano [ADA] supply was staked. The estimated reward rate ranges from 1.30% to 3.00% APY, with Coinbase’s rate at 1.38%. The high-staked supply showed network resilience and strong conviction from holders.

In other news, Node 11.2 is live on the mainnet and has brought core upgrade features. AMBCrypto reported that node configs came in a new “envelope” format that nodes older than 11.2 would not be able to read, which means operators will have to upgrade.

Despite the news, the price reaction has been minimal so far.

The MVRV ratio measures how much profit or loss the average investor is sitting on. Santiment data also tracks MVRV data across different rolling timeframes, such as 30-day (short-term holder) and 180-day (long-term holder).

The data showed that both the 30-day and 180-day MVRV ratios were below zero. In other words, these holders were facing losses, on average, of 15.21% and 8.9%, respectively.

The September rally saw the MVRV ratios reach highly positive territory, especially for the 180-day cohort. It was expected that this would prompt profit-taking and selling pressure. The sustained ADA price losses below $0.20 were also due to crypto market-wide fear as Bitcoin faced rejection below $80k.

The daily active addresses numbers have tumbled compared to levels registered in the first half of October too, from a peak of 29k to just 12k-14k in recent days.

The price correction to $0.194 in mid-September saw the 2-year dormant circulation spike, and also caused a minor dip in the 2-year mean coin age.

These metrics measure the amount of tokens not transacted for a specific period of time, and the average number of days all ADA tokens were in their addresses, in a rolling window of the chosen time period (for example, 90 days).

Over the past six weeks, while Cardano was in steady decline, both the 90-day and 2-year MCA have trended higher. Meanwhile, the dormant circulation was relatively quiet.

This relative quiet was markedly different from the panicked offloading seen in June. Back then, Cardano prices were crashing towards the $0.14-$0.15 lows. Dormant circulation was high, and the mean coin age was in freefall.

Therefore, despite the steady drawdown in recent weeks, the sentiment was likely quite different, as the evidence at hand shows.

Binance Warns iOS Flaw Could Expose Wallet Seed Phrases as Apple’s Latest iPhone Patch Stays Silent

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Binance warned iPhone users Tuesday that a publicly released iOS flaw could let malicious apps read files stored by other apps, including the seed phrases and private keys that control crypto wallets.

“A vulnerability (worse_query) affecting iOS 26.7.1, 27.0.1, and 27.2 allows malicious apps to read local files from other apps, putting seed phrases, private keys, and login credentials at risk across the entire ecosystem,” the exchange wrote on X. “This is an iOS-level issue, not unique to Binance.”

Binance told users to delete any apps installed from untrusted sources, such as sideloaded or manually signed IPA files, and to update iOS and every app to the latest official versions. Anyone who suspects a breach should move funds to a new wallet created on a clean device and reset all active login sessions, the exchange said.

Neither Binance nor the researcher behind the flaw has reported any thefts tied to it.

The bug is a sandbox escape. On iOS, each app is supposed to live in its own container that other apps cannot read. A researcher who posts as forcequitOS released worse_query on GitHub on Nov. 8, calling it “a zero-day sandbox escape for iOS 26 and 27, again.” It succeeds an earlier exploit, bad_query.

The project’s README says it works on iOS 26.0 through 26.7.2 and on iOS 27.2, a wider range than the versions Binance named. It lists paths it can reach, including other apps’ data containers and shared app group folders.

The researcher describes it as “a proof-of-concept for developers” that “isn’t useful by itself,” and stresses it is not a jailbreak. To use it, a developer has to build it into an app.

That has already happened in at least one case. A day after the release, developer Christoph Kolbicz published a file manager built on worse_query that lets users browse and edit app data on iOS 27 without jailbreaking, which he said was tested on iOS 27.0.1.

Blockchain security firm SlowMist called worse_query a container sandbox escape rather than a jailbreak. Its chief information security officer, who posts as 23pds, wrote that the flaw will not steal assets on its own. The risk comes when a user installs and runs an untrusted IPA that then reads other apps’ containers, and only if a wallet stores mnemonic phrases, keystores or WebView caches in plain text on the device.

Apple shipped iOS 27.2.1 and iOS 26.7.3 on Nov. 9, a day after the proof of concept went public. Their security notes list fixes for AirPlay, Bluetooth pairing, brotli and other components, but neither names worse_query or describes a sandbox escape of this kind.

That leaves it unclear whether the latest iOS builds block the technique.

The exposure depends heavily on how each wallet app stores secrets. By SlowMist’s reading, wallets that do not write recovery phrases or keys to plain files on the device are not exposed in the same way, and hardware wallets keep private keys off the phone entirely. The attack also requires a user to install software from outside the App Store first.

The warning comes from an exchange still recovering from its own breach. Binance lost $6.34 billion in an Oct. 29 attack that began with a fake job interview. Security worries have also pushed some large holders to swap self-custodied bitcoin for IBIT shares, while hardware wallet maker Ledger said an organized crime group spent millions targeting its users.

For iPhone users holding bitcoin in a mobile wallet, Binance’s advice amounts to basic hygiene: stay on official iOS releases, skip sideloaded apps and move funds if a device may have been compromised.

All your Apple devices need to be patched ASAP – here’s why

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Security updates for iPhones, iPads, Macs, Apple Watches, and Vision Pro just rolled out.

On Tuesday, Apple released security patches for iOS, iPadOS, MacOS, WatchOS, and VisionOS, which address wireless security flaws in Bluetooth pairing, AirPlay, and Messages.

According to Apple’s security notes, vulnerabilities can allow attackers to establish a Bluetooth connection without user interaction, bypass AirPlay connection rate limits, and join an iMessage group chat and receive its messages. Around half of these flaws were found internally by Apple, while the others were submitted by researchers.

You should update your devices now, as several generations are affected across device categories. The following software versions include patches:

Apple’s security notes list a fix for Messages to prevent attackers from remotely joining an iMessage group chat and reading its messages. According to Apple, “an authorization issue was addressed with improved validation” to fix the issue.

There was likely a flaw in Apple’s iMessage authorization logic that allowed an unauthorized user to inject themselves into a group chat roster without permission from an existing member.

Also: Your iPhone can spot scams for you with iOS 27

Another issue in Messages could allow an authorized added member to access messages within a group chat that were sent before they joined. According to Apple, the code that caused this issue has been removed.

One security fix across devices and software versions addresses an issue where a malicious actor could establish a Bluetooth connection to your device without user interaction.

Apple was not specific about which kind of Bluetooth attack this fix addresses; however, it’s reminiscent of Bluetooth Low Energy Re-Pairing vulnerabilities found earlier this year by French researchers.

This study explains how bad actors can exploit vulnerabilities in Bluetooth Low Energy pairing logic to execute one-click or zero-click impersonation and man-in-the-middle attacks against unsuspecting devices, regardless of Bluetooth version or security level.

Also: Beware of Bluetooth sniffers: Your favorite gadgets could expose you

The study was published in January of this year, and the researchers reported initial findings to Apple, which “[appeared] to have hardened the re-pairing logic in MacOS/iOS 26,” according to the study. It’s possible that Apple internally found another vulnerability in the same Bluetooth pairing logic, given how many devices across Bluetooth versions are affected.

According to Apple, this issue was fixed with “improved checks,” likely meaning strengthened verification steps during pairing.

Another vulnerability pertains to AirPlay, allowing an attacker within Bluetooth range or on the same network to bypass the rate limit on AirPlay pairing code attempts. This issue also affects iOS, iPadOS, MacOS, VisionOS, and WatchOS.

This vulnerability is similar to the AirBorne attack method. This report, published in 2025 by Oligo, describes how zero-click remote code execution, user-interaction bypass, denial-of-service, man-in-the-middle, and other techniques can be used to take control of a device that supports AirPlay.

However, the current security issue is likely less serious or far-reaching and was discovered internally.

For this issue, when you pair a device with an AirPlay receiver, such as an Apple TV, AirPlay-enabled TV, or Mac, it will prompt you for a security PIN to verify your device. You can only input an incorrect PIN a certain number of times before you’re denied pairing for a period of time.

Also: The new Apple TV 4K is always listening for ‘Hey Siri’

A vulnerability in this defense mechanism can allow an attacker within Bluetooth range of, or on the same network as, your device to use a brute-force attack, bypassing the mechanism that limits the number of incorrect PIN attempts, and eventually gain access to your device. There are only so many PIN combinations.

According to Apple, “a logic issue was addressed with improved state management” to fix this problem.

AI is becoming an information gateway. Audiences still don’t want it making their news.

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

An average of 24% of respondents across nine countries said they’d seen a label noting that content was made with AI in the last week, and 20% had seen a bullet-point AI news summary.

In my Nieman Lab prediction this time last year, I wrote that “in 2026, the use of AI as an information gateway will only rise further.”

As it turns out, I was right. In new research that I published with my colleagues Rasmus Kleis Nielsen and Richard Fletcher, one of our many findings is that generative AI systems are becoming ever more significant information intermediaries, both when intentionally used and when incidentally encountered.

For our new report, “Generative AI and News Report 2026: From Standalone Tools to Information Infrastructure,”  we surveyed audiences across nine countries, including the United States, on their use of AI in their everyday lives, what they think its impact will be on different sectors of society, and what they think about its use in news and journalism specifically. I obviously think you should read the whole report, but here are some of the major takeaways.

The use of standalone conversational AI systems such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini continues to grow rapidly, with 51% of respondents across the six countries where we have past data saying they now use AI weekly (49% in the U.S.). That’s a faster spread, at least in the U.S., than smartphones or the internet saw.

Many people also encounter AI through “incidental” AI exposure in the AI-infused services and platforms they’re already using. Search engines such as Google and Bing are key drivers: 57% of respondents across countries said they saw or engaged with AI-generated answers or summaries in the last week. News media, too, drives AI exposure. An average of 24% of respondents across nine countries said they’d seen a label noting that content was made with AI in the last week, and 20% had seen a bullet-point AI news summary.

Despite all the talk about AI agents, we find that, so far, they haven’t broken into the mainstream. The vast majority of respondents across all nine countries (67%) said they had not used an AI agent in the last week. Fourteen percent used one for a real task and 13% tried one just to experiment.

Our data provides solid evidence that a broader transformation of the information environment is afoot. Conversational AI systems have arrived as information intermediaries. Trust is not the main reason people use them. Instead, people see conversational AI systems as friction-reducing machines that get them information more quickly.

“Getting information” continues to be the most common use case. Thirty-eight percent of respondents across countries (35% in the U.S.) said they had used generative AI for getting information in the last week, up from 24% in 2025 and 11% in 2024.

We looked more closely at what people do when they say they use AI for getting information. Across the six countries we’ve surveyed since 2024, we primarily see a mixture of search-equivalent behaviors like “researching a topic” (24%) or “answering factual questions” (18%). People are also increasingly turning to AI for advice (20%).

Using AI directly for news, meanwhile, remains a niche activity, as we demonstrated in the Digital News Report earlier this year. But that use is increasing: 9% of respondents across our six core countries say they use AI for news, up from 6% in 2025 and 3% in 2024.

Many people don’t hold clear or strong views about whether AI output is trustworthy. But people who do rely on it for information trust it. Across the nine countries, 75% of those who used AI to answer factual questions trusted the answers, as did 76% of those who used it for advice and 72% of those who used it for news. Only small minorities expressed distrust.

At the same time, trust and trustworthiness do not, at this stage, seem to be central to why people rely on AI systems. Instead, people tend to see these systems as friction-reducing machines. The convenience of getting a faster and easier answer was chosen by 64% as the main reason they use AI, followed by the interactivity afforded by natural language interfaces (56%). Only a minority (21%) said they use AI because they get more reliable information from it than from other sources.

As AI systems become widely used, their growing popularity is likely to affect the use of legacy content offerings and digital platforms and services. As my coauthor Rasmus Kleis Nielsen put it on LinkedIn, rapid growth in AI use isn’t just eating into what news sites have to offer. It’s eating into search, too — with people who use AI for information saying they use not just news, but search engines less, too, and by a wider margin. Challenging times may be ahead not just for news publishers, but also for the incumbent digital giants previously seen as unassailable.

As in previous years, we wanted to know what people think of AI’s use in journalism and news. We find that people’s attitudes here are remarkably entrenched and negative — though they also haven’t worsened significantly.

People across countries remain uncomfortable with the use of AI in the newsroom, especially with the idea of news being made entirely or primarily with AI. On average across nine countries, just 12% of respondents say they are very or somewhat comfortable with news made entirely by AI. That figure rises to 19% if there is some human oversight. This has hardly changed since 2024, and the gap between human- and AI-made news appears in every country we surveyed.

This also remains true regardless of whether it is breaking news or reporting. People are even more uncomfortable with AI being used for commentary or opinion — formats where the expectation that a human should share their view is strongest.

Our findings raise several important questions for journalists and publishers navigating the rise of generative AI.

First, generative AI is becoming an increasingly significant information intermediaries, with a number of possible knock-on effects on news. People are using AI to reduce friction by getting “good enough” outputs for some information needs — and skipping legacy platforms, established publishers, and the time and effort it takes to find the best available information. Discovery, brand attribution, data collection, and monetization all grow more challenging for publishers in this context.

Second, even though use continues to grow, the public remains skeptical of AI in news. This isn’t just a challenge for newsrooms’ internal adoption. It also shows that publishers using AI need to communicate to audiences what’s in it for them.

Third, beyond news, our findings speak to wider debates about AI’s societal impact. As in other studies on this topic, we find that uptake of AI is uneven. Weekly use is more common among people in professional occupations than those in entry-level or service occupations. Most people rely on free — usually less advanced — AI models.

The differences in how — not just whether — people use AI may become a new dividing line. As AI researcher and OpenAI cofounder Andrej Karpathy recently argued, there is a widening “gap in shared understanding of LLM capability,” with most people not seeing what has left those inside AI firms both excited and worried.

Expectations of what AI will do differ far more between daily users and people who have never used it than between any other groups in our survey. When two groups form very different views of the same technology because they have such different starting points, there will likely be consequences.

This doesn’t matter just for people’s individual choices around whether and how to make use of AI in their own lives. It also matters where AI is adopted and governed through collective decisions — in public services, for example — that apply to those who do not use it.

Felix Simon is the research fellow in AI, information, and news at the Reuters Institute for the Study of Journalism and a research associate at the Oxford Internet Institute (OII) at the University of Oxford.

Enter once, distribute everywhere: Can Open Real Estate deliver?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Open Real Estate is positioning itself as a centralized listing entry and distribution layer for agents, MLSs and portals. Industry voices say the effort could accelerate RESO adoption, but unclear economics and MLS specific data requirements may create added workflow overhead.  AI Summary

No one knows yet what to make of Open Real Estate.  Not even one of the companies asked to help build it.

Ocusell Founder and CEO Hayden Rieveschl said his company has been under an NDA with Open Real Estate for “many, many months.”

Ocusell already offers essentially what Open Real Estate’s founders have stated they want to build. A company FAQ states: “Ocusell gives brands & brokers a single, standardized listing workflow that protects data, ensures compliance and publishes listings across every system in their tech stack and MLS they belong to.”

The company announced an integration with BrightMLS on November 3, the press release for which even referenced Open Real Estate.

“The launch arrives as the Open Real Estate (ORE) brokerage coalition actively conducts a national technology vendor RFP to achieve ‘input once, distribute everywhere’ listing entry. With Ocusell already live across 30 MLSs and OneList, powered by Ocusell, now live at the nation’s largest MLS, Ocusell is uniquely positioned to turn that vision into reality,” the release stated.

Rieveschl’s agreement limits what he can share about the end product, though on that matter he admits he’s also in the dark.

“In regards to what Open RE has pitched, yeah, I mean, essentially what they pitched is a single point of entry with, you know, full status listings, proactive compliance accommodating entirely to, you know, the MLS or multiple MLSs’ rule sets,” he said. “And obviously, Ocusell is the only proven solution that’s been in production for many years with over 30 plus MLSs at this point that can accommodate that request.”

“What’s no secret is that the broker community has wanted this for decades,” he said.

Rieveschl said the founding team — AGNT Inc. (eXp Realty and NextHome), HomeServices of America, Keller Williams Realty and Real REMAX Group — didn’t present him with a fleshed-out vision for a final outcome. “But what I can say is that in all of the meetings I’ve been in with these guys, this has always been complimentary to the MLS.”

The group’s on-the-record comments appear to support Rieveschl, as they’ve been lacing appearances with terms like “seller choice,” “fair, open marketplace,” “transparency” and “fair housing.”

What is clear is that the four CEOs want to put listing data back under control of its producers and those licensed to wield it. And, when a revenue model emerges, the group said it will reward only agents and their brokerages.

A week in and knowing only those few things, Open Real Estate “secured 826 letters of support from real estate brokerage leaders across the country,” according to a HousingWire report. Prominent brands Brown Harris Stevens, Lamacchia Realty, Samson Properties and Windermere Real Estate were announced as part of the initial conglomerate.

However, goodwill and love letters don’t make you money. Nor do they provide actual operational clarity. And an effort this big is going to require a lot of both. So, what exactly is the plan?

On Andrew Flachner’s Playmakers podcast the day after the announcement, founding member and Real REMAX’s CEO Tamir Poleg called Open Real Estate a “data layer.”

“Essentially what Open Real Estate does is create a way for agents to send listings to one place and that place then distributes those listings to MLSs, to portals, to all of the stakeholders in the industry,” Poleg told Flachner. “It’s a data sharing platform on the residential side that serves obviously agents and should obviously benefit consumers.”

HousingWire asked multiple software vendors if the model Poleg described is inherently redundant to what an MLS already provides.

Michael Martin, CEO and Co-Founder of real estate AI platform Sidekick, leaned toward the affirmative.

“Any additional add/edit functionality on top of what’s required by a MLS I would see as added overhead, even if the user experience is better or faster than what agents are already accustomed to,” he said. “A larger question relates to field completeness. ORE may require only a subset of fields to be completed and then upon syndication to local MLSs, additional fields will need to be filled in that are MLS specific. It would be a poor user experience for an agent to have to supplement the information again in their local MLS dashboard.”

Martin said the relationship could sour between ORE and its members’ respective MLS if participating brokerages lean toward more data input that favors portals’ consumer-minded experiences “since the portals have a direct-to-consumer relationship with buyers and sellers,” he said.

“The number of fields displayed by Zillow and Redfin are very different than the underlying MLSs that syndicate to them.”

Flachner is also Co-Founder and CEO of RealScout, a company that pays MLSs to license listing data. He told HousingWire that “Open Real Estate’s greatest impact may be what it pushes MLSs to do.”

“For example, CRMLS has been natively RESO for years, so it would likely process nearly all of a listing’s details from ORE without changing anything. An MLS with its own field set would drop some of those details. If ORE shows agents that their local MLS drops some of their listing details because its fields don’t match with RESO standards, there’s a much greater chance that agents and their brokers will push for the MLS to adopt RESO. This could move the industry toward standardized data faster than anything we’ve seen. Whether MLSs choose to work with ORE or modernize so they don’t need it, the industry ends up with better data.”

Dan Foody, Co-Founder of Cloze, speculated that ORE could become a faster, less-friction prone conduit for data provisioning than what many MLSs offer. It wouldn’t be redundant, per se, but a cleaner, smarter source of property data.

A wide range of industry vendors use data from the MLS ecosystem to power products. Methods vary, but licensed listing data is a revenue source for them and according to Foody, those license agreements can be a hassle.

“So we’ve got one brokerage customer where we have, I think it’s over 50 individual agreements with a single MLS,” Foody said. “We have to manage 55-plus legal agreements. And then, if we want to make a change, we have to modify every single one of those agreements, and read it, resign them all.”

Foody reasons that a vendor could theoretically hook-up to ORE and work with a single provider through a less process-laden workflow.

Flachner said vendors are essential to expanding the reach of these listings, and thinks they should support the effort. “But ORE needs to be structured in a way that encourages their participation and makes economic sense,” he said.

“If Open Real Estate charges significant fees for substantially the same inventory, vendors could end up paying twice for the same listings,” he said. “ORE plans to compensate brokerages for their listings, but if vendors have to pay significant fees to distribute those same listings, those costs ultimately get passed back to agents and brokerages through the tools they use to serve their clients. In effect, brokerages could end up paying for the very distribution they’re being compensated for.”

However, Foody thinks that it’s already too late for both MLSs, ORE and Compass.

“If this is a counterplay to what Compass is doing, then that’s understandable,” he said. “But the whole thing that everyone misses in this … it’s like these little puppies yapping at each other, and then there’s this wolf [named] Google sitting over there salivating. Now there’s all these pools of data that no one has centralized. And guess who’s really good at creating a single way for consumers to search for something? That’s Google.”

Foody thinks the fragmentation Compass is fueling, and now exacerbated by ORE despite its industry-first intention, has all but broken the system.“And so this strategic error, I think, of Compass basically taking stuff off the market, so consumers have to go multiple places. The only winner of that is gonna be Google and the AI vendors. It will not be Compass.”

“I think Google has all of the data on every house. They just need a legally licensed way to provide it,” he said. Google even announced on November 4 that three more brokerages agreed to feed it listings, and it’s a significant haul: REMAX Advantage, Berkshire Hathaway HomeServices Florida Properties Group and ENRG Realty, all RealTrends Verified brokerages.

To arrive at what Open Real Estate might look like, turn to one of the companies not invited — Howard Hanna.

Its CEO, Hoby Hanna, told HousingWire he was a bit surprised to not get the call from the cool kids. After all, Hanna’s company was part of Project Upstream, a now defunct but very similar initiative.

“It would have been nice to be at the table, but obviously those guys didn’t see me at their level,” Hoby Hanna, the CEO of Howard Hanna Real Estate Services, told HousingWire.

In an intriguing bit of irony, Hanna’s company could be already very much involved. In fact, it might be providing ORE’s template.

“We have invested a lot of time and money into the platform we have built, HannaList,” Hanna said. “We are moving forward with that, and we’ve cooperated with MLSs to build it, so I’d really have to understand what the end game is and get a sense of if they can really pull it off before deciding if we will participate.”

HannaList is the company’s multi-step listing marketing approach very similar to Compass’s well publicized “phased marketing” program.

If Ocusell can take in and manage listing data for an 18-state powerhouse brokerage, there’s little reason to not do it for brokerages in all 50. Hanna said he has a great relationship with Ocusell.

“We always try to structure our relationships as a little bit more of a partnership model,” Hanna said. “In this case, we would try to build something that wasn’t just benefiting us as a competitive advantage, but I’ve always thought that what we were investing in was helping them build something that could be good for the industry.”

In that effort, Hanna said he would allow connected MLSs to resell it to other brokerages.

“I’d say that is the product Open RE should build around. I’ve been very happy with it, and our agents are very happy with it. Ocusell has proven they can build it,” said Hanna.

Hanna is curious why the team at Open RE wouldn’t inquire about HannaList, a proven use case for Ocusell’s technology. “I could say, ‘Here are the challenges, here’s where the hiccups could be.’ You know, the timing it takes. Here’s the lift that it takes.”

Regarding Upstream, Hanna said that’s what inspired HannaList. “It’s the exact same concept. I just built it for myself and my agent.”

What’s that they say about original ideas?

Man with ‘demon face syndrome’ sees cat’s eyes in human faces

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 10, 2026 11:32 AM EST

A 28-year-old man got a little bit more than he bargained for after a life-saving brain surgery. Karl (last name withheld for privacy) sees features of his grey tabby cat, Luna, on the human faces he encounters throughout the day. He’ll see Luna’s almond-shaped eye sockets, enlarged pupils, or stripes on humans, if her face is the first one he sees in the morning. Alternatively, he may see human features on the cat if he sees a human face at the beginning of his day.

Karl’s feline ordeal is detailed in a report published in the December 2026 issue of the journal Cortex. He has a form of palinopsia, a group of visual symptoms where an individual keeps seeing something even when it is not present. According to the Cleveland Clinic, palinopsia occurs when something disrupts vision-related processing in the brain. Many conditions and circumstances can cause it including migraine, seizures and epilepsy, strokes, some prescription drugs, and brain bleeds.

In Karl’s case, palinopsia leads to face distortions, a rare condition called prosopometamorphopsia (PMO), sometimes called  “demon face syndrome.” That scary nickname is because the distortions can sometimes look warped and scary like a demon.

Karl’s recurrent palinopsia began two years ago after part of Karl’s right temporal lobe was removed because the blood vessels between the arteries and veins there were tangled. This tangling can lead to a fatal brain bleed if left untreated. The right temporal lobe plays a critical role in face processing, so when Karl’s was partially removed it started messing with his ability to process faces.

“This case of Karl seeing his cat’s features in human faces and vice versa demonstrates that individual features can be pulled from memory and influence our ongoing perception,” Brad Duchaine, a study co-author at cognitive neurologist at Dartmouth College, said in a statement. Duchaine is also the principal investigator of the Social Perception Lab at Dartmouth, which studies these kinds of perception disorders.

After learning about Karl’s experience, Duchaine thought back to a 1986 study also published in Cortex. A woman on a bus saw a poodle on a passenger’s lap, and then saw that poodle’s face on every passenger riding the bus. The woman in that scenario had experienced a stroke nine months before.

However, in Karl’s situation, only components of faces are transferred rather than the entire face. To visually illustrate what Karl was experiencing, Duchaine prepared a sketch that he then fed into a large language model (LLM) to make the image look more realistic. He then shared the image with Karl, and the pair modified a set of images that are included in the report.

“AI provides researchers like us with the ability to generate more accurate visualizations of what people are experiencing when they’re having distorted visual perceptions,” Duchaine explained. “If you know what the person is experiencing, you can much better research what they’re dealing with and try to figure out ways to control it. And now the patient can also take part in that process of creating those visualizations.”

Duchaine’s lab has heard from over 250 people who experience face distortions like these, with some saying they have been told that they have a psychiatric problem. In a few of those cases, patients have been put on antipsychotics, when they are having visual problems, not a psychiatric issue.

The team plans to work with Karl in the future, to further probe when and if his condition changes over time and use similar AI tools to see what he is seeing. Understanding what he is seeing could help researchers better understand how to help people with this condition.

“The use of AI is also helpful for patients who want to convey not just to their healthcare professionals, but to their friends and family, what faces look like to them,” Duchaine concluded.

Disclosure: Ziff Davis, Popular Science’s parent company, filed a lawsuit against OpenAI in April 2025, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.

Google’s new AI tool just proved Signal encryption really works – here’s how

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

With the aim of securing other vital systems, Google DeepMind plans to open-source its AlphaProof Code.

Researchers at Google DeepMind used AI to formally verify portions of Signal‘s underlying code — meaning they’ve mathematically proven that at least parts of the encrypted messaging app’s code do what they say, without errors. That’s reassuring given how many vulnerabilities AI systems have discovered in software lately, and how many people’s communications are protected by Signal’s cryptography. WhatsApp and Google Messages use the same encryption.

Also: How AI security failures are endangering your supply chain

DeepMind used AlphaProof Code, “a novel agentic framework for formal verification at unprecedented depth that combines human expertise with AI agents,” the researchers said. They added that formal verification “is the highest standard of security assurance,” but, without automation at scale like AI systems, difficult and time-consuming.

AI companies like OpenAI have been on a math-breakthrough tear as of late, in some cases solving nearly century-old math problems with AI. By computing at such vast scale, AI models can rapidly accelerate much of the manual work mathematicians do.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, ZDNET’s parent company, filed an April 2025 lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

Using AlphaProof Code and building on earlier verification work by the Signal Foundation, DeepMind researchers divided the verification task into two camps. Humans worked with agents to identify what they needed to prove, and multiple agents running Gemini Carbon, the company’s latest model, then executed it. The agents also audited the proof and “highlighted areas that needed a second look by a human — just 1% of the codebase, a massive time savings,” the researchers wrote.

Also: After the vibe-coding rush comes the debugging hangover

For good measure, researchers tested AlphaProof Code’s ability to distinguish correct from flawed code by showing it two previously fixed Signal bugs, for which it produced correct proofs.

“Our work marks the first time AI has been used to formally verify a widely deployed cryptographic protocol at this depth,” the researchers wrote. “It’s also the first time the implementation of a widely deployed protocol like this has been formally verified by any method, with or without AI.”

Normally, researchers need to manually develop a proof to verify software, which takes much longer. That labor-intensive barrier has made formal verification a distant promised land, until now. The DeepMind team’s verification took a month to do what might normally take experts years.

DeepMind did not verify all of Signal’s code, but noted that this test is a step toward improved security infrastructure at a time when AI is disrupting the entire cybersecurity space.

Also: How to encrypt emails in Gmail, Outlook, and more

“Having the core Signal protocol rigorously verified with automated theorem proving gives us even greater confidence that our code delivers on its privacy guarantees,” the researchers wrote.

The breakthrough reiterates how deeply AI is both a massive risk for cybersecurity and an equally powerful fix. The year 2026 has been especially marked by AI-related security incidents, many of which involve agents. At the same time, companies like Anthropic, Google, and OpenAI are developing models geared specifically toward cybersecurity, including Anthropic’s Claude Mythos.

Also: How to easily encrypt your files on an Android phone

DeepMind did not share any plans to release its findings as a consumer product, though the researchers are open-sourcing their code to help others with formal verification efforts. “Verifying Signal’s core cryptography lays the groundwork to secure other vital systems, from medical device firmware to power grid software,” DeepMind added

October inflation ran hot again, making a December Fed hike likely

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

October CPI increased 0.4% and core CPI rose 0.3% as shelter and energy prices climbed. Economists said the report keeps a December Fed hike in play and could pressure mortgage rates.  AI Summary

Inflation came in hotter than expected in October, further opening the door for the Federal Reserve to raise interest rates at their meeting in December. The Consumer Price Index (CPI) rose 0.4% on a monthly basis in October, according to data released Tuesday by the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS).

The BLS is not releasing an estimate of yearly inflation as the bureau did not collect data last October due to the government shutdown. However, economists estimate that headline annual inflation remains likely in the high 3% range.

Despite coming in hotter than economists had hoped, monthly inflation was down in October, after recording a 0.6% monthly increase in September.

The index for shelter rose 0.3% from a month prior, accounting for over 30% of the monthly all items index increase. This monthly increase is down slightly from a 0.4% jump in September. Within the shelter index, the index for owner’s equivalent rent rose 0.3% month-over-month.

In addition to shelter, the index for food jumped 0.5% from a month prior and the index for energy rose 0.7% from September, down from a 5.2% monthly increase a month ago. This cooler level of inflation for energy comes as the index for gasoline rose just 1.0% month-over-month, compared to 9.1% in September. In addition, the index for electricity rose 0.9%, while the index for natural gas fell 0.8%.

“Price pressures remained broad across goods and services, suggesting businesses across the economy are passing higher costs through to consumers,” Sam Williamson, First American’s senior economist, said in a statement. “At the same time, disinflationary tailwinds from categories such as shelter appear to be fading. With fewer forces helping pull inflation lower, further progress may be harder to come by.”

Core inflation, represented by the all items less food and energy index, rose 0.3% month-over-month, the same as in the prior two months.

Other indexes that increased over the month include recreation (+0.8%), medical care (+0.3) and motor vehicle insurance (+0.7%). In contrast, the index for used cars and trucks (-1.2%) and the index for communication (-1.0%) were among the small number of major indexes that fell in October.

According to economists, this hotter-than-expected report puts growing pressure on the Federal Reserve to act after they left interest rates unchanged at their last meeting.

“With the labor market showing remarkable resilience and inflation moving in the wrong direction, policymakers have fewer reasons to hold back. That keeps a December rate hike firmly on the table, a move that would further unwind the insurance cuts delivered late last year,” Williamson said.

Notably, even prior to the BLS releasing the CPI data, in a speech Tuesday morning Fed Governor Chirstopher Wallace put a 50 basis point move on the table for December.

“Additional hikes in early 2027 look more in play as well, with the 10-year Treasury up nine basis points just 25 minutes after the CPI report release,” Jake Krimmel, the senior economist at Realtor.com, said in a statement. “For housing in the near term, we could see upward pressure on mortgage rates again. After three straight weekly declines to 7.18%, rising Treasury yields and growing speculation about hikes in early 2027 could reverse that trend. October’s spike to 7.4% showed how quickly higher rates hurt demand.”

While this could delay the plans of some prospective homebuyers, Williamson notes that stronger job growth could give some households the income they need to facilitate the move, but he agrees with Krimmel that higher mortgage rates will be a hurdle for all buyers.

“Until mortgage rates ease, life moves will keep the housing market moving, while discretionary buyers wait on the sidelines,” he said.

Open Real Estate pitches a listing data layer, but vendors cite MLS specific fields, compliance friction and cost overlap risks.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/ai-and-machine-learning/google-cloud-rolls-out-gemini-healthcare" hreflang="en">Google Cloud rolls out Gemini for healthcare</a>

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Google Cloud rolls out Gemini for healthcare" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Google launched Tuesday Gemini for healthcare, an agentic artificial intelligence platform aimed at connecting complex clinical, financial and administrative workflows used by healthcare providers and payers.

The platform was developed alongside clinical and health plan leaders and is built to support HIPAA compliance, the tech giant said.

“The fundamental challenge in healthcare isn't a lack of information—it's the immense friction of making that data actionable across a highly regulated ecosystem," said Thomas Kurian, Google Cloud CEO, in a statement. “By working directly alongside our healthcare customers, we've built a platform that resolves chronic bottlenecks so that the industry can put its focus entirely back on human health.”

There are four main components to the “integrated, fully governed” system: skills for healthcare work, connectors to various administrative systems, open healthcare partner ecosystem and a secure, governed platform with open model choice.

It directly integrates with electronic health records (EHRs), billing systems, security and compliance platforms, clinical guidelines and workflows, coding, enterprise productivity platforms and Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) datasets.

Gemini for healthcare is available in preview as of Nov. 10. Gemini Enterprise Standard and Gemini Enterprise Plus customers will be able to access the platform, and can choose between per-user subscriptions and pay-as-you-go pricing.

Dozens of companies from all corners of the industry are partnering with Google on the launch, including GoodRx, Epic, IMO Health, Oracle Health, Infinitus, Suki and more.

Infinitus CEO Ankit Jain told Fierce Healthcare the opportunity for healthcare AI is “much bigger than making existing workflows faster or more efficient.”

“It's about fundamentally changing what's possible—enabling healthcare organizations to support more patients, move care forward faster, and deliver at a scale that wasn't possible before,” Jain said. “But that requires AI that can do more than answer questions. It needs to take action, navigate complex systems, and handle the administrative work that too often gets in the way of patients getting care.”

Jain continued, “That's what this partnership with Google Cloud represents. By bringing Infinitus agents into Gemini for healthcare, we're making it easier for healthcare teams to put AI to work on the processes that connect patients to care. It's another step toward a future where more people get the care they need, without the complexity of the healthcare system standing in their way.”

Several major health systems have also teamed up with Google for the launch, including HCA Healthcare and Northwell Health. Other organizations include Blue Cross Blue Shield of Massachusetts, American Society of Clinical Oncology and Highmark Health.

“Making it easier for physicians to access and understand a patient’s medical history helps them spend less time gathering information and more time focused on patient care,” said Michael Schlosser, M.D., HCA Healthcare EVP and chief clinical officer, in a statement. “Through our work with Google Cloud, we’re exploring how Gemini can interpret and synthesize complex patient information from multiple electronic health record systems and care settings, connecting data from different sources to give clinicians a more complete and cohesive understanding of a patient’s medical history.”

10-year Treasury yield races back to 5% as inflation data, Waller comments drive rate hike expectations

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Treasury yields jumped on Tuesday after the U.S. consumer price index data for October came in hotter than expected.

The yield on the 10-year Treasury note — the benchmark for mortgages, car loans and credit card rates — climbed more than 14 basis points to 5.004%. The 30-year Treasury bond yield, more sensitive to geopolitical developments, rose more than 10 basis points at 5.364%. The 2-year Treasury note yield, which more closely follows short-term Fed rate decisions, added more than 20 basis points to 4.755%.

The 10-year yield last traded above the 5% level earlier this month. Back in October, the 10-year and 30-year yields hit fresh 24-year highs.

One basis point equals 0.01% or 1/100th of 1%, and yields and prices move inversely to one another.

The consumer price index for October grew 0.4%, more than the 0.3% expected from economists polled by Dow Jones and down from the 0.6% growth in September.

Core CPI, which excludes volatile food and energy prices, gained 0.3%. Economists had expected a 0.2% gain on the month. The reading was in line with September's 0.3% rise.

The report came after Fed Governor Christopher Waller said he would support "more robust action" if inflation readings are higher than expected.

"In that case, I believe a 50 basis point hike in the policy rate should be considered at the December meeting," Waller said in London at a central bank conference hosted by the Bank of England. "Hiking only 25 basis points would put our credibility at risk, a risk I am not eager to take."

Lindsay Rosner, head of multi-sector fixed income investing at Goldman Sachs Asset Management, said the hot CPI numbers signal that Fed hikes could spill into 2027.

"The Fed could push for a more-aggressive tightening cycle with CPI continuing to print strong," she said. "A December hike is our firm base case despite wider AI jitters, and stubborn inflation means we cannot rule out further hikes in 2027."

Oil prices dipped further on Tuesday morning, with Brent crude falling below $90 a barrel for the first time since August. This comes on the back of President Donald Trump's comments that the war with Iran could end "fairly soon" as he played down the prospect of further strikes in the region.

Tuesday's CPI data, together with wholesale inflation numbers due Friday, may shape the Federal Reserve's future decisions on interest rates in the U.S. Markets have already largely priced in a 25 basis point hike in December, according to CME Group's FedWatch.

It comes after U.S. employment data came in considerably stronger than Wall Street was expecting last week, with 165,000 jobs added in October, more than double forecasts.

FDA Initiates Labeling Changes for GLP-1s Due to Rare But Serious Eye Disease Risk

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

GLP-1 users have a new symptom to watch for: Sudden vision loss in one eye.

The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) announced on November 9 that it will require drugmakers to add information to the warning labels of Ozempic, Wegovy, Mounjaro, Zepbound and other GLP-1 medications about “a potential increased risk” of a rare eye condition called nonarteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION).GLP-1 medications are approved to treat type 2 diabetes and to help people lose weight and keep it off.NAION joins a growing list of side effects linked to GLP-1s, adding a second eye warning to several labels.The U.S. labels for Ozempic, Rybelsus, Trulicity, Mounjaro, and Zepbound already warn about diabetic retinopathy, a diabetes-related eye disease, according to each drug’s prescribing information.The two conditions affect different parts of the eye. Diabetic retinopathy damages tiny blood vessels in the retina, the layer at the back of the eye that senses light, according to the National Eye Institute. NAION damages the optic nerve when it doesn’t get enough blood, according to the FDA’s announcement.Mihail Zilbermint, MD, director of endocrine hospitalists at Johns Hopkins, told Healthline he hasn’t seen NAION in his patients. He has seen vision worsen in some people after they start a GLP-1.“But we believe that’s probably related to rapid improvement in their sugar control, which can also be seen in someone who is starting insulin,” Zilbermint said.

A 2024 study published in JAMA Ophthalmology “initially triggered” the FDA’s review, the agency told Healthline.

The study found a higher risk of NAION in people prescribed semaglutide, the active ingredient in Ozempic and Wegovy, than in people prescribed other diabetes or weight-loss drugs.The authors wrote that more research is needed to determine whether semaglutide causes NAION.Joseph F. Rizzo III, MD, director of the Neuro-Ophthalmology Service at Mass Eye and Ear in Boston, served as the study’s corresponding author.

In a 2024 podcast, Rizzo said he and a resident saw three patients with NAION who were taking semaglutide for about a week.“And because NAION is a fairly uncommon disorder in the general population, the fact that we had three within a relatively short period of time led me to suspect that there was a higher risk of developing NAION in patients on semaglutide,” Rizzo said. The FDA analyzed clinical trials for multiple GLP-1 drugs and noted that NAION cases were reported with every GLP-1 on the market, according to its announcement. Those findings led the FDA to require the warning for the entire drug class, the agency told Healthline.

About 70% to 80% of people with NAION first notice vision loss when they wake up, according to StatPearls. The FDA listed these symptoms:

No treatment has been proven to reverse vision loss from NAION, according to StatPearls.

“Patients experiencing sudden, painless vision loss in one eye should contact their healthcare professional immediately and should not stop their medication without consulting their provider first,” the FDA told Healthline.

In the announcement, the agency told doctors to promptly refer patients with suspected NAION to an ophthalmologist.

The FDA listed these possible risk factors for NAION:

The FDA told Healthline that many people who take GLP-1s also have these conditions, which made reports of NAION in GLP-1 users “challenging” to interpret.

Across 126 clinical trials, the FDA found about 6 NAION cases per 100,000 people taking a GLP-1 for a year, and about 4 per 100,000 people taking a placebo or another diabetes drug for a year.In real-world data, NAION was more common with GLP-1s than with one type of diabetes pill, but the FDA found no significant increase compared with another type, the agency told Healthline.Zilbermint said patients who saw the news have worried about losing their vision. He tells them that “many of the serious harms, including the potential harm of NAION, are rare.”“The overall risk is still very small,” he said.

Regulators in other countries have added NAION warnings, mostly for semaglutide:

The FDA told Healthline the benefits of GLP-1s “continue to outweigh their risks when used as directed.”The FDA has also approved some GLP-1s to lower the risk of heart attack and stroke, reduce the risk of worsening kidney disease, and treat sleep apnea and a type of liver disease.Zilbermint said that “for most of my patients with obesity, diabetes and heart disease, the benefits clearly outweigh the risks.”“But we definitely have to decide patient by patient,” he added.

The labeling change applies to these medications, according to the FDA’s announcement:

As of publication, the FDA’s online label database did not yet show the new warning on the drugs’ labels.
