The 7 Airlines Flying Airbus A321XLRs On Both Short & Long-Haul Routes

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In evolutionary biology, conversation is often focused on 'the' thing features evolved for. For example, bird wings evolved for flying. In reality, birds will use them for whatever they can. Penguins use them for swimming, while chickens use them for changing direction while running and shading their chicks.

Aircraft are somewhat similar. They may be optimized for a specific mission, but that doesn't mean they will be used for those missions by the airline all the time. In Vietnam, VietJet sometimes flies its widebody A330s on ultra-short domestic routes from Ho Chi Minh City to Nha Trang half empty. VietJet apparently does this because the time slot is available in the A330's international schedule.

The Airbus A321XLR is optimized for longer, but thinner routes. On shorter routes, its increased weight from its extra tanks and strengthened landing gear imposes a penalty. Even so, some airlines will operate them on short routes, partly because they are available. Let's examine some of the airlines using the XLR on both short and longer-haul routes.

Aer Lingus is the flag carrier of Ireland and has a fleet of six Airbus A321XLRs. These are configured with 16 business class seats and 168 economy class seats for a total of 184 seats. This is identical to its complementary fleet of eight A321LRs. In July 2026, Aer Lingus flew more than 300 flights longer than 3,100 miles (5,000 kilometers) and more than 200 routes shorter than 1,250 miles (2,000 kilometers) with its XLRs.

Those shorter routes are European hops when the aircraft are not flying their longer transatlantic trips. An example of a long Aer Lingus XLR flight is Dublin to Nashville, which comes with a scheduled flight time of nine hours and 20 minutes. Meanwhile, during the first week of November, the XLR (registered EI-XLU) has flown hops such as Dublin to London (48 minutes) and Dublin to Barcelona (one hour and 50 minutes).

Flightradar24 shows EI-XLU's longer recent flights include Nashville (Tennessee), Cleveland (Ohio), Toronto (Canada), Indianapolis (Indiana), and New York. Being located at the far western edge of Europe, Aer Lingus has the advantage of being able to reach more destinations in the US with the XLR. The XLR is an excellent aircraft for thinner transatlantic missions, but it is also ok to use on short hops, accepting a modest penalty.

Iberia was the launch customer for the Airbus A321XLR in late 2024, and today it operates a fleet of seven examples with an eighth on order. These are configured with 14 business class seats and 168 economy seats, totaling 182 seats. Spain (excluding Russia) leads Europe with the most domestic flights, driven by the need to connect the mainland with the Canary Islands and Balearic Islands.

From its base in Madrid, Iberia flies its A321XLRs to transatlantic destinations in the United States such as Boston, Newark, Washington DC, and Canada's Toronto. Having a more Spanish and Latin focus than carriers like the UK or Ireland, it also flies them to the Dominican Republic (Santo Domingo) and Puerto Rico (San Juan) in the Caribbean (both Spanish-speaking).

In South America, its XLRs fly as far as Recife and Fortaleza in Brazil. Recent flight tracking data for the XLR registered EC-OOJ reveals it is mostly flying longer transatlantic routes. However, it has also flown European hops to Zurich and Paris (both one hour and 30 minutes) as well as Venice (two hours).

Qantas will have one of the highest passenger capacities onboard its Airbus A321XLRs.

Air Canada is one of the XLR's newest operators, with the national Canadian airline flying the aircraft since June. Now the airline has a growing fleet of five to six in service. Air Canada's official website says it has six in inventory, while Planespotters.net still records five delivered, of which one is currently grounded. That aircraft is brand new and working up.

Regardless, Air Canada is in the process of building up one of the world's largest XLR fleets, with around 24 of its 30-strong order still to be delivered. Air Canada is using the aircraft on a range of longer routes, including transatlantic flights to Europe. C-GXLR was the first XLR delivered to Air Canada. In recent weeks, its longest flight was Toronto to Copenhagen (up to eight hours),

Other longer routes include Montreal to Toulouse (about seven hours), as well as Montreal to Lisbon, Montreal to Porto, and other European destinations. It is also used extensively on medium (three- to five-hour) flights to various destinations in the United States and western Canada such as Los Angeles and Calgary. Ultra short hops include Ottawa to Toronto (39 minutes) and Montreal to Toronto (53 minutes).

Qantas is another of the leading Airbus A321XLR customers, with a total of 48 on order or delivered. The Flying Kangaroo is currently taking delivery of its XLRs, with seven now in inventory. These are ideally suited to some Pacific routes where the range is vast, but the routes are thin. The fact that Qantas ordered 20 more XLRs in August 2025 suggests it has many Asia-Pacific routes in mind.

However, few have been announced. The XLR registered VH-OGA was the first delivered to Qantas in April 2025. Confusingly, its flight history over the last month shows it has only been used on domestic routes. The shortest route is the one-hour flight between Sydney and Melbourne, while the longest flight is the domestic transcontinental flight from Sydney to Perth, which is less than five hours.

Qantas is introducing the type progressively and assigning it to domestic trunk routes before expanding internationally. Upcoming international routes include Perth to India (expected), Adelaide to Singapore, and Brisbane to Manila. While Qantas has not announced all of its upcoming Pacific routes, interesting destinations include Fiji, Samoa, the Cook Islands, and Tahiti.

American Airlines now has 11 of its 40 XLRs ordered in inventory. As with European carriers, American is assigning these to transatlantic routes. They also have the range to reach many leisure and thinner destinations in the Caribbean, Central America, and South America.

The first XLR American took delivery of is N300NY. Its most recent flight history is dominated by transcontinental routes flying from Boston or New York to Los Angeles or San Francisco, taking around five hours. It is also flying transatlantic routes from New York to Barcelona (up to eight hours) and New York to Edinburgh (up to six hours).

Largest publicly reported A321XLR customers

While short hops appear less common in American's fleet, there are some. One XLR (N301NY) has flown hops like Boston to Philadelphia (one hour). As with other airlines, the American XLR fleet is working up and is yet to take on many of its intended routes. South America is identified as a future market with potential American routes including Miami to Porto Alegre, Brasilia, Belo Horizonte, and others.

The Airbus321XLR will change the mid-sized market and allow new destinations for airlines.

United Airlines is another major airline busy inducting its new XLR fleet and gearing up for transatlantic and other routes. It has recently suggested that the XLR lacks the range to serve some deeper European routes, such as those to Greece. This is somewhat confusing, as it is well within the advertised range and other airlines fly comparable distances.

United has not provided a detailed breakdown of its calculations, including payload, required margins, routing, etc., to understand the assertion. It has just received the first three of its 50-strong order, leaving 47 remaining on the order book. These are now starting to replace its aging fleet of 757-200s.

With the first delivered in May, United's XLR remains a fleet in its infancy. Flight records show they have yet to fly longer transatlantic routes to Western Europe, although they are coming. Mostly, the flights have been transcontinental, around four to five hours. One short hop in the record is San Diego to Denver, a flight time of less than two hours.

IndiGo is the leading low-cost carrier of India and is one of the world's fastest-rising carriers. It boasts a fleet of 425 aircraft in service and has 881 more on order. While delivered, that will make it one of the largest airlines in the world in the 2030s. The airline's fleet is overwhelmingly A320 family narrowbodies, although it has 60 A350 widebodies on order.

IndiGo has 175 A321neos in service plus four new XLRs. It has the largest order for the XLR, totalling 69 examples. This is made up of the 40 original firm orders publicly confirmed by IndiGo and 29 additional aircraft reflected in industry fleet databases. Once again, the fleet is in its infancy and is working up.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

With that being said, IndiGo seems much faster at deploying them on its longer-haul routes than other airlines. Flight history for VT-NLA shows it has been flying between Delhi and Athens (over eight hours), Mumbai and Istanbul (over seven hours), Mumbai and Amsterdam (up to nine hours and 30 minutes). One example of a short hop is the two-hour route between Delhi and Mumbai.

'Godzilla Minus Zero' Exposes the Biggest Problem with Christopher Nolan's World War II Epic

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Director Takashi Yamazaki made his new Godzilla movie in direct response to Christopher Nolan's film

In 2023, two Oscar-winning blockbuster movies tackled the topic of nuclear weapons and the bombing of Hiroshima and Nagasaki in very different ways. Christopher Nolan's Oppenheimer told the story of the atomic bomb through the eyes of its creator, J. Robert Oppenheimer (Cillian Murphy), while Takashi Yamazaki's Godzilla Minus One continued the 70-year cinematic tradition of using a giant radioactive monster as a metaphor for nuclear warfare.

Notably, neither movie actually depicted those nuclear explosions or their immediate aftermath onscreen, but three years later, one of those directors has done exactly that — and it's not Nolan.

Light spoilers ahead for the plot of Godzilla Minus Zero.

While Nolan followed up Oppenheimer with The Odyssey, which in many ways feels like a spiritual sequel about another brilliant man whose actions changed the course of history (arguably for the worse), Yamazaki delivered a direct sequel in Godzilla Minus Zero. The film, which is currently stomping its way across movie theaters worldwide and dominating the U.S. box office, features a brutal depiction of the immediate aftermath of the bombing of Hiroshima.

Just before the climax of the film, we see a flashback in which one bloodied and stunned character stumbles through the streets of the obliterated city. The camera then points directly at the charred remains of several humans who once lived there. Yamazaki juxtaposes this directly with images of Godzilla's destruction in perhaps the most unsubtle metaphor in a movie released this year.

With that one scene, Yamazaki pulls off the one thing Nolan was never brave enough to do: he shows the actual, awful carnage that fuels both director's Oscar-winning movies. And as it turns out, this isn't a coincidence.

Back in March 2024, right after winning an Oscar for Best Visual Effects, Yamazaki responded to a question comparing his and Nolan's movies with an intriguing promise.

“As a person of Japanese ancestry and descent, my response to Oppenheimer [is that] I would like to dedicate a different film to that when that day comes,” Yamazaki told the press.

Speaking to Time Magazine more recently, Yamazaki offered a bit more insight into how Godzilla Minus Zero became that response.

"In my mind I was thinking I'd develop a film that looks directly at Hiroshima," he said. "But then I began thinking: Wait — Godzilla is itself a metaphor for nuclear war. Maybe I can combine the two and make a Godzilla movie that is also an answer to Oppenheimer."

Godzilla is itself a metaphor for nuclear war.

The result is a stunning cinematic sequence that's in direct conversation with Nolan's film while also pushing the legacy of the Godzilla franchise to a bold new place it's never ventured before. For Yamazaki, however, it's not just about one-upping other movies, it's about showing the horrors of nuclear warfare to a modern audience that might otherwise never see them.

"It was important to show as many people as possible what the effects of an atomic bomb would really look like," he said. "I wanted to evoke something similar because it's very important for it to feel relatable — to jog our imaginations in a way that says, 'Wow. This is real. It is a very violent act.'"

Nolan devoted much of Oppenheimer to exploring the brutal reality of nuclear war, but he never went so far as to actually show us what happened in Japan. The director did offer an explanation for why at the time, though it's not particularly satisfying — especially in the wake of Godzilla Minus Zero.

At a public event in 2024, Nolan explained why he decided not to show the actual bombing of Hiroshima and Nagasaki in his movie.

"The feeling for me as a filmmaker was very strongly that to depart from Oppenheimer’s experience would betray the terms of the storytelling," Nolan said. "He learned about the bombings of Hiroshima and Nagasaki on the radio — the same as the rest of the world."

It's true that Oppenheimer is told from the main character's perspective, but there is one exception. While most of the film is essentially Oppenheimer's POV, several black-and-white sequences intercut throughout show us the same series of events from the perspective of antagonist Lewis Strauss (Robert Downey Jr.), including some events that Oppenheimer never witnessed first-hand.

So it's not like showing Hiroshima or Nagasaki would have broken the rules of Oppenheimer set out by Nolan, it's just that he chose not to do it. Of course, that's a valid choice, even if it's arguably one of the film's only flaws. But with the benefit of hindsight after seeing how Yamazaki depicts that horrifying event — and how just a few seconds of screentime hits harder than Nolan's entire movie — it's hard not to look back at Oppenheimer with a sense of disappointment.

Maybe after watching Godzilla Minus Zero, Nolan himself will feel a similar sense of regret, but considering the director almost never makes sequels, I doubt he'll revisit the events of World War II and the bombing of Hiroshima and Nagasaki any time soon. Thankfully, we have Yamazaki's brilliant Godzilla sequel, which might not even exist if it wasn't for Oppenheimer in the first place.

Set up t212-autopilot on the demo account, tested against a fake broker

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

First thing: t212-autopilot doesn't make money. Its own README says the signals it was built around averaged −0.187R over 213 picks, and the one pattern that looked positive got all of it from a single trade. That's why it defaults to the demo account, a £100 ceiling and only 10% on picks. It's a tool for running a plan you've already proved, not a way to make one work. Not financial advice.

With that said, I've written a proper setup guide, and it's tested every week.

You can't let CI near a real brokerage account, demo or not. So the check runs the real CLI against a fake Trading 212 API that answers the reads the CLI makes and records orders instead of placing them. That lets it prove the safety rules, not just that the code imports:

Generate the key on the demo account. A live key gets 401 from the demo host, and that 401 nearly always means the wrong account, not a bad key.

The guide explains the limit you'll hit first: Trading 212's public API has no price endpoint, so ETFs you don't already hold get skipped. Full guide: docs/SETUP.md.

Has anyone actually found an edge they'd automate with real money? Genuine question.

Coulisses 03 spore donner une voix aux applications

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Une application ne peut pas seulement agir. Elle doit confirmer, informer et prévenir. Lorsque les premières applications de la Fabrique ont eu besoin d'envoyer leurs propres messages, les limites des services externes sont rapidement apparues. Spore est né pour reprendre cette fonction essentielle.

Une application peut fonctionner parfaitement et rester muette.

Un compte est créé, mais rien ne le confirme. Un paiement passe, mais aucun reçu n'arrive. Un mot de passe doit être réinitialisé, mais le lien n'est jamais envoyé. Une erreur survient, mais personne n'est prévenu.

L'e-mail n'est pas un accessoire ajouté à la fin.

C'est la capacité d'une application à s'exprimer : confirmer une action, transmettre une information et prévenir lorsqu'une attention est nécessaire.

Sans cette capacité, la relation entre l'application et son utilisateur reste incomplète.

Avec les premières applications sont arrivés les premiers services externes à payer.

Les API d'envoi d'e-mails sont très attirantes au départ. L'intégration est rapide, les premiers messages sont souvent gratuits et l'infrastructure semble disparaître derrière une simple requête.

Pour un projet qui commence, c'est exactement ce qu'il faut.

Les messages deviennent plus nombreux. Une deuxième application arrive, puis une troisième. Il faut ajouter des domaines, suivre de nouveaux quotas et passer aux offres payantes.

Chaque application apporte alors son abonnement, sa console, sa clé d'API, ses limites et sa facture.

Nous ne payions pas pour une nouvelle capacité. Nous repayions, application après application, pour leur permettre d'accomplir la même fonction : envoyer un message.

Le problème n'était pas que ces services fonctionnaient mal.

Leur offre gratuite permettait de commencer rapidement. Mais une fois l'envoi d'e-mails intégré au cœur de l'application, changer de service devenait plus difficile.

Ce qui n'était au départ qu'une commodité était devenu une dépendance.

À chaque nouvelle application, nous pouvions recommencer : ouvrir un compte, configurer un domaine, intégrer une nouvelle API et accepter un nouveau tarif.

Ou construire cette capacité une fois, puis la partager entre toutes les applications.

Spore est né pour donner aux applications de la Fabrique une infrastructure d'envoi commune.

Une application lui confie un message. Spore s'occupe du domaine, de l'identité d'envoi, de la signature et de son acheminement.

Synthiz peut envoyer un lien de connexion. Lungor, une facture. Nakoda, une alerte. Chaque nouvelle application bénéficie de la même capacité sans devoir reconstruire toute la chaîne.

L'objectif n'est pas d'envoyer davantage d'e-mails.

Il est de mieux envoyer ceux qui sont nécessaires au fonctionnement d'un service.

Une application qui ne peut pas parler dépend toujours de quelqu'un d'autre pour exister auprès de ses utilisateurs.

Envoyer est facile. Arriver est difficile.

Construire un serveur capable d'expédier un message n'était que le début.

L'e-mail repose sur des standards ouverts : SMTP, SPF, DKIM, DMARC. En théorie, toute infrastructure qui respecte ces règles peut communiquer avec les autres.

En pratique, quelques grands fournisseurs contrôlent l'essentiel des boîtes de réception. Ils évaluent les adresses IP, les domaines et les comportements d'envoi selon leurs propres règles.

Une configuration correcte ne garantit donc pas la délivrabilité.

Microsoft a un jour bloqué tout notre sous-réseau alors que notre réputation et notre configuration étaient propres. Pendant trois jours, nos messages ont été refusés avant même d'entrer.

Cette difficulté n'a pas remis Spore en question. Elle a montré pourquoi il devait exister.

Maîtriser son infrastructure d'envoi, ce n'est pas supprimer tous les intermédiaires. C'est comprendre la chaîne, mesurer ce qui se passe et pouvoir agir lorsqu'un message n'arrive plus.

Spore ne repose pas sur une recette secrète.

Son fonctionnement s'appuie sur les standards publics qui organisent la messagerie : transport des messages, signature des domaines, politiques d'authentification, retours d'erreur et réputation des expéditeurs.

Nous rassemblons les textes que nous suivons dans notre littérature technique.

Un service d'e-mail sobre, éthique et souverain, sur les choix qui structurent Spore ; Le jour où Microsoft a bloqué tout notre sous-réseau, sur la réalité de la délivrabilité face aux grandes plateformes.

Skalpai avait donné aux applications un endroit où fonctionner.

Synthiz avait donné forme au premier besoin.

Désormais, chaque nouvelle application peut confirmer, informer et prévenir sans repartir de zéro ni ajouter une nouvelle dépendance.

Mais une application ne doit pas seulement pouvoir parler. Pour durer, elle doit aussi pouvoir encaisser.

Quelques textes par an, rien d'autre. Pas de relance, pas de promotion.

No-Bake Cranberry Orange Bliss Bites

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Disclosure: This post may contain affiliate links which provide us a small commission when used for purchase. We're grateful for your support!

Cozy, bright, and bursting with holiday flavor — these Cranberry Orange Bliss Bites are the perfect little treat when holiday baking season is in full swing and you’re out of time and energy. Inspired by Starbucks’ Bliss Bars, they pack the tart-sweet magic of cranberries and fresh orange zest into tender, no-bake bites you can whip up in minutes!

Even better? They’re vegan, gluten-free, and require zero baking — making them a lifesaver during the busy holiday season. A drizzle of white chocolate (or creamy coconut butter) makes them extra festive, but they’re just as delicious on their own. Let’s make bliss bites!

Besides tasting absolutely magical, these bliss bites get their name because they’re inspired by the legendary Bliss Bars from Starbucks (IYKYK!). The Starbucks bars have a chewy blondie base with cream cheese frosting, cranberries, white chocolate, and orange zest — total swoon!

The addition of orange juice + zest and dried cranberries kick things up a notch and sends them into Bliss Bar territory!

The final touch is an optional drizzle of melted white chocolate chips, coconut butter, or our favorite — a mix of the two! While the white chocolate creates a thicker, sweeter, and more decadent frosting, the coconut butter creates a drippier texture and adds a more pronounced coconut flavor that we enjoy. The combination is the best of both worlds!

We hope you LOVE these bites! They’re:ZestyBrightSweet with pops of tartnessDecadent yet wholesomeQuick + easy to make& SO delicious!

These are the perfect little treat to make during the fall and winter months. They’ve got you covered whether you want something on hand to satisfy sweet cravings during the work week or need a quick healthier dessert to bring to a gathering.

The Sky Today on Tuesday, November 10: Seek Saturn’s two-toned moon

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for a sky event this week? Check out our full Sky This Week column.

November 9: Chase Comet 161P/Hartley-IRAS

Challenge yourself to pick up Saturn’s moon Iapetus, which tonight reaches greatest eastern elongation 9.4’ east of the ringed planet. The two-toned moon is tidally locked to its parent world, which means it rotates in time with its orbit. At eastern elongation, Iapetus’ darker hemisphere faces earthward, reducing the moon’s magnitude to nearly 12. This, combined with its distance from Saturn, makes it more of a challenge to find, but you can give it a try if you’ve got a larger scope.

Try around 9 P.M. local time when Saturn is highest, some 50° above the southern horizon. It’s the brightest point of light in this region of the sky, hanging just below Pisces and to the far upper left of Fomalhaut, the brightest star in Piscis Austrinus (much closer to the horizon). Even if you can’t locate Iapetus or don’t have a large scope, Saturn is magnificent through a telescope. Its disk spans 19” and its rings are roughly 43” across, showing off their southern side. You should definitely spot its largest and brightest moon, mid-8th-magnitude Titan, just over 2’ west of the planet. Depending on your scope and when you look, you may also spot at least three other moons: 10th-magnitude Tethys, Dione, and Rhea, closer to the planet. Tethys lies to Saturn’s west, while Rhea and Dione are to the east.

Sunrise: 6:39 A.M.Sunset: 4:48 P.M.Moonrise: 8:15 A.M.Moonset: 5:19 P.M.Moon Phase: Waxing crescent (4%)*Times for sunrise, sunset, moonrise, and moonset are given in local time from 40° N 90° W. The Moon’s illumination is given at 10 P.M. local time from the same location.

Venus reaches a stationary point among the stars of Virgo at 10 A.M. EST on November 11. This brings its retrograde (westward) motion to an end; after today, Venus will begin moving eastward or prograde through the sky, swinging north of Spica.

You can easily catch the bright planet in the eastern sky before dawn. Venus’ brightness has increased over just the past few days, and it now blazes at magnitude –4.7. An hour before sunrise, you’ll easily spot it alongside Spica, standing just to the star’s upper right. Both are 10° high and rising as dawn approaches. Check out Venus through a telescope: its disk shows off a crescent phase just 10% lit, but stretches an impressive 53” in the sky.

Also just becoming visible at this time is Mercury, barely 1.5° above the horizon and glowing at magnitude 1.2. See if you can locate it about 13° east (to the lower left) of Venus, although you’ll need a particularly flat horizon free of trees, buildings, and lights. Mercury, too, rises as twilight brightens, which may make the planet harder to see in the growing light. Try scanning for it with binoculars, although make sure to put them away well before sunrise from your location

Alison Klesman is senior editor of Astronomy magazine. She holds a Ph.D. in astronomy and has studied a variety of topics, from minor planets to supermassive black holes.
